🌏 IELTS Đọc · 6 bài mới (v123) (6)

Bài học thuật Claude soạn cho 7 dạng câu còn thiếu (Y/N/NG, tìm đoạn, nối đặc điểm, nối nửa câu, tóm tắt, sơ đồ, trả lời ngắn). Kiểm: đáp án đúng và duy nhất, YES / NO / NOT GIVEN đúng ý tác giả, người / số liệu trong bài là hư cấu nhưng hợp lý.

Paper from the Fields B2
Bài

A. Every year, farmers around the world harvest crops such as wheat, rice and sugar cane, and every year they are left with enormous quantities of stalks, husks and leaves. In many regions this material is simply burned in the open, a practice that clears the fields quickly but fills the air with smoke. In northern India, for example, the burning of rice stubble after the autumn harvest has been linked to serious air pollution in cities hundreds of kilometres away. Researchers and businesses are now asking whether this so-called agricultural waste could be turned into something useful instead: paper. B. The idea is not new. Before wood became the main raw material for paper in the nineteenth century, paper makers relied on rags, cotton and plant fibres. Wood won because forests offered a cheap and seemingly endless supply, and because large mills could process logs efficiently. Today, however, the pressure on forests, together with the cost of transporting timber over long distances, has made other sources of fibre attractive again. Straw from wheat and rice, and bagasse, the fibrous material left after sugar cane has been crushed, are the most promising. C. Turning straw into paper involves several stages. First, the straw is collected from the fields and stored in dry conditions, because damp straw quickly begins to rot. At the mill it is cut into short pieces and cleaned to remove soil and stones. The pieces are then cooked in a solution of water and chemicals, which breaks down the lignin, the natural glue that holds plant fibres together. The result is a soft mass called pulp. The pulp is washed and, if white paper is required, bleached. Finally, it is spread in a thin layer over a moving screen, where the water drains away and the fibres bond to form a continuous sheet, which is pressed and dried. D. Straw has some clear advantages over wood. Its fibres are shorter and the material contains less lignin, so it needs less energy and fewer chemicals to cook. A crop of wheat is also ready within a year, whereas a pine plantation may take twenty-five years to reach a size that can be harvested. For farmers, selling straw provides an extra source of income from land they already use. E. There are, however, significant difficulties. Straw is bulky, which makes it expensive to transport, so mills must be built close to farming areas. Supply is seasonal, meaning that a mill has to store enough material to keep running for the rest of the year. Straw also contains silica, a hard mineral that damages machinery and makes it harder to recover the chemicals used in cooking. Because of the short fibres, paper made only from straw tends to be weaker, and manufacturers often mix it with a proportion of wood pulp. F. Despite these problems, interest is growing. Several mills in China already produce paper mainly from straw, and a number of small companies in Europe now sell notebooks and packaging made from agricultural residues. Supporters argue that the greatest benefit may be environmental rather than commercial: every tonne of straw that is made into paper is a tonne that is not burned in the fields.


• a comparison of how long two raw materials take to grow → đoạn D (Đoạn D: lúa mì sẵn sàng trong một năm, rừng thông có thể mất 25 năm.)
• a reason why straw paper mills must be located near farms → đoạn E (Đoạn E: rơm cồng kềnh, chở đắt nên nhà máy phải gần vùng trồng trọt.)
• an example of a health problem linked to a farming practice → đoạn A (Đoạn A: đốt gốc rạ ở miền bắc Ấn Độ gây ô nhiễm không khí nặng.)
• an explanation of why one raw material replaced others in the past → đoạn B (Đoạn B: gỗ thắng vì rừng cho nguồn rẻ, nhà máy lớn xử lý gỗ hiệu quả.)
• examples of straw products that can already be bought → đoạn F (Đoạn F: công ty nhỏ ở châu Âu bán sổ tay và bao bì làm từ phụ phẩm nông nghiệp.)
• Straw collected and stored in ______ conditions → dry (Đoạn C: stored in dry conditions, because damp straw quickly begins to rot.)
• Pieces cooked in water and chemicals to break down the ______ → lignin (Đoạn C: breaks down the lignin, the natural glue…)
• Pulp washed and, for white paper, ______ → bleached (Đoạn C: if white paper is required, bleached.)
• Pulp spread over a moving ______ so the water drains away → screen (Đoạn C: spread in a thin layer over a moving screen.)
• What is the fibrous material left after sugar cane is crushed called? → bagasse (Đoạn B: bagasse, the fibrous material left after sugar cane has been crushed.)
• How many years may a pine plantation need before it can be harvested? → twenty-five / 25 / twenty-five years / 25 years (Đoạn D: may take twenty-five years.)
• Which hard mineral in straw damages machinery? → silica (Đoạn E: silica, a hard mineral that damages machinery.)
• In which country do several mills already make paper mainly from straw? → China (Đoạn F: Several mills in China…)
Shorter Weeks, Better Work? C1
Bài

A. The five-day working week is so familiar that it is easy to forget it was once a radical idea. When some factories adopted it in the early twentieth century, critics warned that shorter weeks would ruin businesses. Instead, productivity rose and the two-day weekend became the norm across much of the industrialised world. A century later, a growing number of employers are experimenting with a further reduction, to four days, and the debate has an oddly familiar ring. B. The most widely reported experiments follow a simple model. Staff work four days instead of five, are paid the same salary, and are expected to maintain the same output. In one recent trial involving around sixty companies, most of the firms chose to continue with the shorter week after the six-month test period ended. Managers reported that sick days fell and that fewer employees resigned, while revenue remained broadly stable. C. I find these results encouraging, but I am wary of the enthusiasm with which they are sometimes presented. The companies that volunteered for trials were, almost by definition, those that expected the change to work. Many were small offices in sectors such as marketing and software, where output is hard to measure and where meetings can be cut without obvious harm. It would be a mistake to assume that a hospital, a school or a bus company could simply follow their example. D. That said, the critics' favourite argument, that people will simply do less work, seems to me weak. Studies of office workers have repeatedly found that only a fraction of an eight-hour day is spent on focused work; the rest disappears into interruptions, unnecessary meetings and checking messages. When time is limited, people become noticeably more careful about how they use it. The shorter week, in other words, may work less by reducing effort than by reducing waste. E. The more serious risk, in my view, is that the extra day off comes at the price of longer and more intense working days. Some employees in the trials described the remaining four days as exhausting, and there is evidence that very long shifts increase errors and accidents. A four-day week built on four ten-hour days is not the same reform as one built on four eight-hour days, and it should not be sold as such. F. What the experiments do show, I believe, is that the length of the working week is a choice rather than a law of nature. Whether a particular organisation should reduce it depends on the kind of work it does, and that is a question best answered by careful trials rather than by slogans from either side.


• The results of the recent trials give some cause for optimism. → YES (Đoạn C: I find these results encouraging.)
• The companies in the trials were typical of employers in general. → NO (Đoạn C: công ty tự nguyện là những công ty tin sẽ thành công; nhiều là văn phòng nhỏ.)
• The argument that staff will do less work in a shorter week is convincing. → NO (Đoạn D: seems to me weak.)
• Most office workers spend the majority of their day on focused work. → NO (Đoạn D: only a fraction of an eight-hour day is spent on focused work.)
• A four-day week made up of ten-hour days should be described differently. → YES (Đoạn E: should not be sold as such.)
• The four-day week will become as common as the two-day weekend within a decade. → NOT GIVEN (Tác giả không dự đoán về thời gian phổ biến.)
• In the trial involving around sixty companies, most firms … → decided to keep the four-day week after the test. (Đoạn B: most of the firms chose to continue with the shorter week.)
• According to the writer, a shorter week may succeed mainly because people … → waste less of their working time. (Đoạn D: by reducing waste.)
• Some employees found that the remaining four working days … → left them feeling very tired. (Đoạn E: described the remaining four days as exhausting.)
• The writer believes that the decision to shorten the week should … → depend on the type of work involved. (Đoạn F: depends on the kind of work it does.)
• Tóm tắt (1) → interruptions (Đoạn D: the rest disappears into interruptions…)
• Tóm tắt (2) → meetings (Đoạn D: unnecessary meetings)
• Tóm tắt (3) → careful (Đoạn D: more careful about how they use it)
• Tóm tắt (4) → waste (Đoạn D: by reducing waste)
Why Do We Yawn? B2
Bài

A. Almost every vertebrate animal yawns, from fish and snakes to dogs and humans, yet the purpose of this ancient behaviour remains surprisingly unclear. A yawn lasts around six seconds on average and involves opening the mouth wide, taking a deep breath and then breathing out more slowly. Humans start yawning before they are born: ultrasound scans have shown babies yawning in the womb from about eleven weeks after conception. B. For many years the most popular explanation was that people yawn when they need more oxygen. This idea, however, has not survived testing. In a series of experiments, psychologist Dr Anna Kowalski asked volunteers to breathe air containing different levels of oxygen and carbon dioxide. Changing the mixture had no effect on how often they yawned. 'If yawning were about oxygen,' she notes, 'we would expect people in stuffy rooms to yawn constantly, and they do not.' C. A more recent theory links yawning to the temperature of the brain. Professor James Okafor argues that a yawn works like a radiator: the deep breath and the stretching of the jaw increase blood flow to the head and bring in cooler air, which lowers brain temperature slightly. In support of this, his team found that people yawned less often when they held a cold pack against their forehead, and that yawning was more frequent in spring and autumn than in hot summer weather, when the outside air is too warm to have a cooling effect. D. Yawning is also famously contagious. Seeing, hearing or even reading about a yawn can trigger one, and roughly half of adults will yawn after watching a video of someone else doing so. Dr Mei Lin, who studies social behaviour, has found that people are more likely to catch a yawn from a family member than from a stranger. She believes this pattern suggests that contagious yawning is connected with empathy, the ability to share the feelings of others. E. Not everyone accepts this interpretation. Dr Lucas Ferreira points out that children under the age of four rarely catch yawns, even though they can clearly respond to other people's emotions. In his view, contagious yawning depends mainly on attention: we catch yawns from people we are watching closely, and we tend to watch our relatives more than strangers. He also notes that dogs can catch yawns from their owners, which he considers evidence that the behaviour does not require complex understanding. F. Whatever its purpose, yawning appears to be useful enough to have survived millions of years of evolution. Researchers agree that further studies, particularly of animals that live in groups, could finally explain why we find it so hard not to yawn when others do.


• Catching a yawn depends on how closely we watch someone. → Dr Lucas Ferreira (Đoạn E: Ferreira: depends mainly on attention.)
• Yawning helps to lower the temperature of the brain. → Professor James Okafor (Đoạn C: Okafor: a yawn works like a radiator.)
• The amount of oxygen in the air does not change how often people yawn. → Dr Anna Kowalski (Đoạn B: Kowalski: changing the mixture had no effect.)
• People catch yawns more easily from relatives than from strangers. → Dr Mei Lin (Đoạn D: Mei Lin: more likely from a family member.)
• Some animals can catch yawns from humans. → Dr Lucas Ferreira (Đoạn E: Ferreira: dogs can catch yawns from their owners.)
• Tóm tắt (1) → radiator (Đoạn C: works like a radiator)
• Tóm tắt (2) → blood (Đoạn C: increase blood flow to the head)
• Tóm tắt (3) → forehead (Đoạn C: a cold pack against their forehead)
• Tóm tắt (4) → summer (Đoạn C: than in hot summer weather)
• Tóm tắt (5) → warm (Đoạn C: the outside air is too warm)
• How long does a yawn last on average? → six seconds / around six seconds / 6 seconds (Đoạn A: around six seconds on average.)
• How many weeks after conception have babies been seen yawning? → eleven / about eleven / eleven weeks / 11 (Đoạn A: from about eleven weeks after conception.)
• What proportion of adults yawn after watching a video of someone yawning? → half / roughly half (Đoạn D: roughly half of adults.)
Fresh Water from the Sea C1
Bài

A. More than two billion people live in areas where fresh water is scarce, and the problem is likely to grow as populations increase and rainfall patterns change. Yet the planet is not short of water; it is short of water that can be drunk. Around 97 per cent of the world's water is in the oceans, and turning it into drinking water, a process known as desalination, has become one of the fastest-growing industries in the world. There are now more than 20,000 desalination plants in operation, many of them in the Middle East, North Africa and Australia. B. The oldest method imitates the natural water cycle. Seawater is heated until it evaporates, and the steam is then cooled so that it condenses as pure water, leaving the salt behind. This approach, called thermal distillation, is reliable but uses enormous amounts of energy, which is why it has mainly been built in countries with cheap oil and gas. C. Most new plants use a different technique, reverse osmosis. Here, seawater is first taken in through pipes placed some distance from the shore, where the water is cleaner. It is passed through coarse filters that remove sand, seaweed and small sea creatures. Next, high-pressure pumps force the water against a membrane, a thin sheet of plastic with holes so tiny that water molecules can pass through but salt cannot. The water that emerges is extremely pure, so minerals such as calcium are added before it is sent to homes, both to improve the taste and to protect the pipes from corrosion. D. Reverse osmosis requires far less energy than distillation, and the membranes have improved greatly since the 1980s. Even so, desalinated water typically costs two to three times more than water taken from rivers or underground sources. Energy accounts for roughly a third of the cost, which means that the price of water is closely tied to the price of electricity. E. The environmental effects are also a concern. For every litre of fresh water produced, a plant generates about one and a half litres of brine, water that is roughly twice as salty as normal seawater and may contain chemicals used to clean the membranes. If this brine is released in one place, it sinks to the sea floor and can harm plants and animals that cannot tolerate such high levels of salt. Engineers now use long outfall pipes with many small openings to spread the brine over a wider area, which helps it to mix more quickly. F. Supporters of desalination argue that these problems can be managed, especially if plants are powered by solar or wind energy. Critics reply that reducing leaks from old water pipes and reusing treated wastewater would provide fresh water more cheaply. Most water engineers agree that desalination is not a complete solution, but that in coastal regions with little rainfall it is likely to play an increasingly important role.


• a description of how waste from desalination plants is now released → đoạn E (Đoạn E: long outfall pipes with many small openings spread the brine.)
• a reason why one method has been used mainly in certain countries → đoạn B (Đoạn B: tốn nhiều năng lượng nên chủ yếu ở nước có dầu khí rẻ.)
• the proportion of the cost of desalinated water that is due to energy → đoạn D (Đoạn D: Energy accounts for roughly a third of the cost.)
• other ways of providing fresh water that some people prefer → đoạn F (Đoạn F: giảm rò rỉ ống cũ, tái sử dụng nước thải đã xử lý.)
• Pipes that take in seawater are placed away from the shore because … → the water there is cleaner. (Đoạn C: some distance from the shore, where the water is cleaner.)
• Minerals are added to desalinated water to … → improve its taste and protect the pipes. (Đoạn C: to improve the taste and to protect the pipes from corrosion.)
• Because energy is a major cost, the price of desalinated water … → is closely linked to the price of electricity. (Đoạn D: closely tied to the price of electricity.)
• Brine released in a single place … → sinks to the sea floor and can harm sea life. (Đoạn E: it sinks to the sea floor and can harm plants and animals.)
• Supporters believe the problems of desalination … → could be reduced by using solar or wind power. (Đoạn F: can be managed, especially if plants are powered by solar or wind energy.)
• Seawater taken in through ______ placed away from the shore → pipes (Đoạn C: taken in through pipes placed some distance from the shore.)
• Coarse ______ remove sand, seaweed and small sea creatures → filters (Đoạn C: coarse filters that remove sand…)
• High-pressure ______ force the water against a membrane → pumps (Đoạn C: high-pressure pumps force the water…)
• Minerals such as ______ added before the water is sent to homes → calcium (Đoạn C: minerals such as calcium are added.)
Letting Nature Take Over C1
Bài

A. Across Europe, an idea that would have seemed absurd fifty years ago is gaining support: that the best way to restore damaged landscapes may be to step back and let nature manage itself. This approach, known as rewilding, aims to restore natural processes such as grazing, flooding and the movement of predators, rather than to protect particular species or preserve a landscape exactly as it is. In my view, it is one of the most promising developments in conservation, although its supporters sometimes promise more than it can deliver. B. Traditional conservation has often worked like gardening. Reserves are managed carefully, with paid staff cutting back unwanted plants, controlling water levels and protecting rare birds. This can be highly effective for individual species, but it is expensive and never finished. Rewilding asks a different question: which missing parts of the ecosystem, if returned, would allow it to look after itself? C. Often the answer is large animals. Ecologist Dr Sofia Brandt has studied areas where wild cattle and horses were introduced to abandoned farmland. Their grazing, she found, created a mosaic of open ground and scrub that supported far more insects and birds than either dense forest or regular grassland. Dr Tomas Hale, a hydrologist, has focused on beavers, whose dams slow the flow of streams. At his study sites, beaver ponds held back so much water that flooding downstream was reduced during heavy rain. D. Reintroducing a species is rarely simple. A typical programme begins with a study of whether enough suitable habitat remains. If it does, consultation with local people follows, because farmers and landowners may fear losses. The animals are then usually kept in a large enclosure for several weeks so that they can adjust to their new surroundings before being released. After release, they are tracked, often with radio collars, to check whether they survive and breed. E. Such caution is justified. Economist Dr Ruth Okoye has shown that the costs of reintroductions, such as damage to crops, attacks on sheep and the time spent on consultation, usually fall on a small number of rural households, while the benefits, such as tourism and cleaner rivers, are spread across society. Unless farmers are properly compensated, she argues, rewilding projects will continue to meet strong local opposition. I find this argument convincing, and too many enthusiasts dismiss it as mere resistance to change. F. Rewilding will not save every threatened species, and some habitats, such as traditional hay meadows, actually depend on human management. But where large areas of land are no longer farmed, it offers a cheaper and more self-sustaining alternative to endless management. That, rather than a romantic wish to return to some imagined wilderness, is the strongest argument in its favour.


• Rewilding is among the most hopeful recent ideas in conservation. → YES (Đoạn A: one of the most promising developments in conservation.)
• Supporters of rewilding are always realistic about what it can achieve. → NO (Đoạn A: sometimes promise more than it can deliver.)
• People who oppose rewilding are simply resisting change. → NO (Đoạn E: tác giả phê phán việc gạt đi lý lẽ này là 'chỉ chống thay đổi'.)
• Rewilded areas attract more tourists than traditional reserves. → NOT GIVEN (Bài không so sánh số khách du lịch.)
• One animal reduced the risk of flooding. → Dr Tomas Hale (Đoạn C: Hale: beaver ponds… flooding downstream was reduced.)
• The costs of reintroducing animals are unevenly shared. → Dr Ruth Okoye (Đoạn E: Okoye: costs fall on a small number of households.)
• Grazing animals increased the variety of wildlife. → Dr Sofia Brandt (Đoạn C: Brandt: supported far more insects and birds.)
• Study whether enough suitable ______ remains → habitat (Đoạn D: whether enough suitable habitat remains.)
• ______ with local people → consultation (Đoạn D: consultation with local people follows.)
• Animals kept in a large ______ for several weeks → enclosure (Đoạn D: kept in a large enclosure.)
• Animals tracked with radio ______ → collars (Đoạn D: tracked, often with radio collars.)
• Apart from wild cattle, which animals were introduced to abandoned farmland? → horses / wild horses (Đoạn C: wild cattle and horses.)
• What do beavers build that slows the flow of streams? → dams (Đoạn C: whose dams slow the flow of streams.)
• Which type of habitat depends on human management? → hay meadows / traditional hay meadows (Đoạn F: traditional hay meadows.)
Trees in the City C1
Bài

A. For most of the twentieth century, city trees were treated as decoration: pleasant to look at, but easily removed when a road needed widening or a building needed light. That attitude is changing rapidly. Researchers now describe urban trees as infrastructure, comparable to drains or power lines, because of the services they provide, and I believe this shift in thinking is long overdue. B. The most obvious service is shade. On hot days, a street lined with mature trees can be several degrees cooler than a street without them, partly because leaves block sunlight and partly because trees release water vapour. Climatologist Dr Hannah Reyes measured surface temperatures across one southern European city and found that pavements under trees were up to 15 degrees cooler than exposed ones in the afternoon. C. Trees also influence health in less direct ways. Public health researcher Dr Kwame Asante compared hospital records in neighbourhoods with different amounts of tree cover and found fewer admissions for heat-related illness where cover was highest. He is careful, however, to point out that greener neighbourhoods also tend to be wealthier, so tree cover alone cannot explain the difference. D. The effect of trees on air quality is more complicated than is often claimed. Leaves do trap some particles of pollution, but in narrow streets lined with tall buildings a dense canopy can also prevent polluted air from escaping, raising concentrations at street level. Environmental engineer Dr Lena Fischer recommends low hedges rather than tall trees beside busy roads in such streets. Her work suggests that planting must be planned street by street rather than simply maximised. E. Then there is the question of cost. A young tree may cost several hundred pounds to buy and plant, and it needs watering and protection for its first years. Many city councils, facing tight budgets, plant cheaply and then fail to maintain what they have planted, with the result that a large share of new trees die within a few years. In my view, a city that plants a thousand trees and loses half of them has achieved less than one that plants five hundred and looks after them properly. F. None of this means that cities should stop planting. It means that trees should be chosen, placed and maintained with the same care given to any other piece of public infrastructure. If that happens, the benefits, cooler streets, better health and more pleasant places to live, are likely to be substantial.


• Urban trees deserve to be treated as seriously as other public services. → YES (Đoạn A: this shift in thinking is long overdue; đoạn F.)
• Planting as many trees as possible is the best policy. → NO (Đoạn E: trồng 1.000 cây chết một nửa kém hơn trồng 500 cây chăm tốt.)
• Most city residents would prefer to live in a street with trees. → NOT GIVEN (Bài không nói ý thích của cư dân.)
• a reason why one researcher urges caution about his own findings → đoạn C (Đoạn C: Asante: khu nhiều cây cũng giàu hơn, cây xanh không giải thích hết.)
• a suggestion that a different type of planting may suit some streets → đoạn D (Đoạn D: Fischer khuyên trồng hàng rào thấp thay cây cao.)
• a description of how city trees were regarded in the past → đoạn A (Đoạn A: cây được coi là đồ trang trí.)
• Trees can make air pollution worse in certain streets. → Dr Lena Fischer (Đoạn D: Fischer.)
• Shaded pavements can be much cooler than unshaded ones. → Dr Hannah Reyes (Đoạn B: Reyes: up to 15 degrees cooler.)
• Differences in wealth may partly explain differences in health between areas. → Dr Kwame Asante (Đoạn C: Asante.)
• Trees cool the air partly because they … → release water vapour. (Đoạn B: partly because trees release water vapour.)
• Many new trees die within a few years because councils … → fail to look after them. (Đoạn E: fail to maintain what they have planted.)
• Tóm tắt (1) → particles (Đoạn D: trap some particles of pollution)
• Tóm tắt (2) → canopy (Đoạn D: a dense canopy)
• Tóm tắt (3) → maximised (Đoạn D: rather than simply maximised)

🎓 Cambridge Đọc · bài mới (v123) (22)

My favourite place to relax A2 · nối
Bài

A Lena There is a small lake near my town, and I go there on Sunday mornings when it is quiet. I usually take a book and sit under a big tree. Sometimes my brother comes with me and we go fishing, but we never catch much! In winter it is too cold, so I go to the library instead. The best thing about the lake is that it is free. B Tom My favourite place is my grandmother's garden. She grows vegetables and lots of flowers, and I help her every Saturday. We often cook lunch together with the vegetables we pick. It takes me forty minutes to get there by bus, but I don't mind because I listen to music on the way. My friends think gardening is boring, but I find it relaxing. C Mai I relax at the swimming pool in the city centre. I go three times a week, usually in the evening after work. It costs five pounds each time, which is quite expensive, so I want to buy a monthly ticket. After swimming I always feel tired but happy. I sometimes meet my friend Kim there and we have a coffee in the café.

Books to read B1 · nối
Bài

A The Silent Forest A thriller set in a dark Canadian forest, where a young detective searches for a missing scientist. The story moves fast and every chapter ends with a surprise. At over 500 pages, it is a long read, but most readers can't put it down. B Kitchen Stories Part cookbook, part family history. The writer shares her grandmother's recipes from Vietnam together with stories about growing up in a busy restaurant. The recipes are simple and the photos are beautiful. C Running Further An experienced athlete explains how anyone can train for their first marathon. Clear weekly plans, advice on food and sleep, and honest stories about the writer's own mistakes make this a practical guide. D Stars Above Us A short, easy introduction to astronomy for teenagers. Colourful pictures and simple diagrams explain the planets, the stars and how telescopes work. Each chapter is only a few pages long. E The Last Summer A gentle, funny novel about three old friends who spend one last summer together in a seaside town. Nothing very dramatic happens, but the characters are wonderful and the ending is moving. F Money Made Simple A clear guide to managing your money, written for young people starting their first job. It explains saving, bank accounts and how to avoid debt without using complicated language. G Lost Cities The true story of the archaeologists who discovered ancient cities in the jungle. Full of adventure and real photographs, it reads like a novel, but every word is true. H Laugh Out Loud A collection of short, funny stories that you can read in five minutes each. It is perfect for reading on the bus or before bed, and a great present for anyone who needs cheering up.


• Minh enjoys exciting stories full of surprises, and he doesn't mind long books. → A The Silent Forest (A: every chapter ends with a surprise; over 500 pages.)
• Rosa has just started her first job and wants to understand how to look after her money. → F Money Made Simple (F: for young people starting their first job.)

My Year as a Volunteer B1 · bài dài
Bài

After I finished school, I didn't know what I wanted to study at university, so I decided to take a year off. My parents were worried that I would waste the time, but a friend of my mother's told me about a project that needed volunteers to help in a small library in the mountains in the north of the country. I had never lived away from home before, and I wasn't sure I would be any good at it, but I agreed to go for six months. The first few weeks were difficult. The village was much colder than I had expected, and I had brought the wrong clothes. The library was just one room in the old school, and most of the books were in very bad condition. On my first day only two children came in, and they left after ten minutes because there was nothing they wanted to read. I realised that I had to do more than sit behind a desk. I started a reading club on Saturday mornings and asked the children what they would like to read. Most of them wanted comics and books about animals, which the library didn't have. So I wrote to publishers and bookshops in the city, and to my surprise several of them sent boxes of books for free. Soon the room was full every weekend, and some parents began to come too. In the end I stayed for a whole year instead of six months. When I left, the village organised a small party for me, and one of the girls from the reading club gave me a card that said she wanted to be a teacher. That was the moment I knew what I wanted to do with my own life. I'm now in my second year of a teaching degree, and I still visit the village every summer.


• Why did the writer decide to take a year off? → She had not decided what to study. (I didn't know what I wanted to study at university.)
• What problem did the writer have when she first arrived? → She had not packed suitable clothing. (I had brought the wrong clothes.)
• Why did the two children leave the library on her first day? → The library had nothing that interested them. (there was nothing they wanted to read.)
• How did the writer get new books for the library? → She asked companies to give them. (I wrote to publishers and bookshops… several of them sent boxes of books for free.)
• What helped the writer decide on her future career? → a message from a child she had helped (a girl… gave me a card that said she wanted to be a teacher. That was the moment I knew.)
The Secret Life of Lost Property B2 · bài dài
Bài

Every day, thousands of people leave something behind on a train, a bus or an underground platform. Umbrellas are the most common items, followed by phones, bags and, more surprisingly, single shoes. Most of these objects end up in a lost property office, and the one I visited last month, which serves a large city's transport network, receives around a thousand items every single day. The office is in a basement beneath one of the main stations, and it looks more like a warehouse than an office. Long rows of shelves stretch into the distance, each labelled with a date. Items are kept for three months; if nobody claims them, they are sold at auction or given to charity. 'People think we're a place where things go to be forgotten,' says Maria, who has worked there for eleven years. 'But we reunite a lot of people with things that really matter to them.' Technology has changed the job considerably. Until recently, staff wrote a description of each item by hand, and anyone who had lost something had to come in person or phone and describe it. Now every item is photographed and entered into an online database, and the system automatically suggests matches when someone reports a loss. As a result, the proportion of items returned to their owners has almost doubled in five years. The work follows a strict routine. Each morning, everything collected from trains and stations the night before arrives in large plastic boxes. Staff sort the items into categories, attach a numbered label and record exactly where and when each one was found, which can be vital when two people claim the same object. Valuable items such as jewellery and cash are counted twice, by two different members of staff, and kept in a safe until their owners appear. Some of the objects that arrive are remarkable. Maria has seen a stuffed bear wearing a crown, a full set of false teeth and, on one occasion, a wedding dress still in its bag. 'The bride came for it the next morning,' she tells me, 'two days before the wedding. She cried when she saw it.' The strangest item she remembers is a small boat, which someone apparently carried onto a late-night train and then forgot. Not everything is so charming. Staff must deal with a lot of food left in bags, and phones and laptops require special care because of the personal information they contain. Electronic devices are locked in a separate room, and anyone claiming one has to prove that it belongs to them, usually by unlocking it in front of a member of staff. What strikes me most is how emotional the work can be. A lost umbrella is easily replaced, but a bag containing a family photograph or a notebook full of someone's writing is not. Maria says the best part of the job is the moment when people realise they are going to get their belongings back. 'You see the relief on their faces,' she says. 'For a few seconds, they forget whatever else is worrying them.'


• What does the writer find surprising about the items people lose? → how many single shoes are left behind (followed by phones, bags and, more surprisingly, single shoes.)
• What does Maria suggest about the lost property office? → People have the wrong idea about what it does. ('People think we're a place where things go to be forgotten… But we reunite a lot of people…')
• What has been the main effect of the new online system? → More items are returned to their owners. (the proportion of items returned… has almost doubled.)
• Why does Maria mention the wedding dress? → to describe a particularly moving return (She cried when she saw it.)
• Why must people prove that a phone or laptop is theirs? → because the devices contain private information (because of the personal information they contain.)
• What is the writer's main point in the final paragraph? → The value of some lost items cannot be measured in money. (a bag containing a family photograph… is not [easily replaced].)
Learning to Fly at Fifty B2 · bài dài
Bài

For most of my adult life I told people that I would learn to fly 'one day'. It was the kind of ambition that is easy to keep because you never have to test it. I had a demanding job, two children and a mortgage, and flying lessons seemed both expensive and slightly ridiculous for someone like me. Then, a few weeks after my fiftieth birthday, my daughter gave me a voucher for a trial lesson at a small airfield outside the city. 'You keep talking about it,' she said. 'Now you have to do it.' I expected the instructor to be a young former air force pilot. In fact Joan was sixty-three and had started flying herself at the age of forty-five, after her children had left home. She spent the first half hour on the ground, walking me round the aircraft and explaining the checks that every pilot makes before take-off. I was impatient to get into the air, but I later realised that this was the most important lesson of all: in flying, preparation is everything. When we finally took off, Joan let me hold the controls almost immediately. What surprised me was not the view, although that was beautiful, but how much the small plane moved in the wind. I gripped the controls so tightly that my hands ached. Joan told me, gently, that the aircraft would fly itself quite happily if I let it, and that my job was to make small corrections rather than fight it. I signed up for a full course that afternoon. The next eighteen months were harder than I had imagined. The written exams covered weather, navigation and air law, and I had not sat an exam since university. There were weeks when bad weather cancelled every lesson, and I began to wonder whether I was too old to learn something so new. Several times I considered giving up. What kept me going was the moment, about halfway through, when I made my first landing without Joan touching the controls. Flying also changed the way I organised my life. Lessons had to be booked around the weather, which meant checking forecasts obsessively and keeping my weekends free. My husband joked that he had lost me to the sky, yet he came to the airfield more often than he would admit, and eventually he took a trial lesson himself. At work, colleagues who had never shown much interest in my private life started asking every Monday whether I had been up in the air. I passed my final test last spring. I now fly about once a month, usually with a friend who shares the cost. I am not a natural pilot, and I never will be, but I have discovered that being careful and methodical matters far more than talent. Perhaps the most valuable thing flying has taught me has nothing to do with aircraft: it is that the excuses we make for not trying things are usually more frightening than the things themselves.


• Why does the writer say that the ambition was 'easy to keep'? → She never had to find out whether she could do it. (you never have to test it.)
• What surprised the writer about her instructor? → She had also started flying in middle age. (Joan was sixty-three and had started flying… at the age of forty-five.)
• What did the writer later realise about the first half hour of the lesson? → It taught her the most important principle of flying. (this was the most important lesson of all: in flying, preparation is everything.)
• What advice did Joan give the writer during the flight? → to stop trying to control the plane so much (make small corrections rather than fight it.)
• What helped the writer to continue the course? → landing the plane on her own for the first time (What kept me going was… my first landing without Joan touching the controls.)
• What does the writer conclude about learning to fly? → Care and method are more important than natural ability. (being careful and methodical matters far more than talent.)
Starting Young: Four Entrepreneurs B2 · nối
Bài

A Jade, 23, runs a company that sells second-hand clothes online I started by selling my own old clothes to pay for a holiday, and I was amazed how quickly they sold. Within a year I had a small warehouse and two employees. The hardest part was learning to say no: friends kept asking me to stock things that didn't fit the business. My advice is to know exactly who your customers are. I still check every item myself before it goes online, because one bad review can cost you dozens of sales. These days I spend more time on photography and packaging than on buying stock, because presentation is what customers notice first. I'd like to open a real shop one day, but rents in my city are far too high at the moment. B Sam, 25, makes and sells skateboards My parents thought I was wasting my time, which made me more determined to prove them wrong. I had no money, so I borrowed tools from a local workshop and built the first boards in my garage. My biggest mistake was growing too fast: I took on a huge order from a shop chain, couldn't deliver on time and nearly went bankrupt. Now I grow slowly and only promise what I know I can do. Each board takes about six hours to make by hand, and no two are exactly the same. Some of my customers are professional skaters, which still amazes me. C Nadia, 22, has developed a language-learning app I built the first version of the app while I was still at university, mainly to help my grandmother learn English. A lecturer encouraged me to enter a competition for student businesses, and winning it gave me the money and contacts I needed. I work with three friends from my course, and we share every decision equally. The app now has thousands of users, but it still doesn't make a profit, which keeps me awake at night. We spend most of our time answering emails from users and fixing the problems they report. Our next step is to add Spanish and French, which users ask for every week. D Ollie, 24, owns a café that employs young people who have left care I wanted a business that did some good, not just made money. Every member of staff is someone who grew up in care and found it hard to get a first job. The café struggled at the beginning because the location was poor, so we moved after a year, and that changed everything. People sometimes assume we survive on donations, but we pay our way like any other café, and I'm proud of that. Training new staff takes time and patience, and not everyone stays. But several former employees now run cafés of their own, which matters more to me than any award.

• Which person started the business to help a family member? → C Nadia (Nadia: mainly to help my grandmother learn English.)
• Which person makes decisions together with business partners? → C Nadia (Nadia: we share every decision equally.)
• Which person has not yet made any profit? → C Nadia (Nadia: it still doesn't make a profit.)
• Which person checks the quality of products personally? → A Jade (Jade: I still check every item myself.)

A Year Abroad: Four Volunteers B2 · nối
Bài

A Linh, who taught English in a village school in Nepal I arrived thinking I would be the teacher, but I learned far more than my pupils did. The school had no electricity for most of the day, so I had to plan lessons that needed nothing but a blackboard and our voices. The families I stayed with treated me like a daughter, and leaving them was the saddest day of the year. I've been back twice since then, and next summer I'm taking my parents to meet them. The children loved learning songs, so we sang in English every morning before lessons. I also learned enough Nepali to chat with the shopkeepers in the village. B Marcus, who worked on a turtle conservation project in Costa Rica Most of the work was at night, walking the beach for hours to protect turtle nests from people who steal the eggs. It was exhausting and sometimes boring, and I almost went home after the first month. What changed my mind was seeing hundreds of baby turtles reach the sea safely. The project made me realise I wanted to study biology, which is what I'm doing now. The volunteers came from more than ten countries, and we lived together in a simple wooden house near the beach. Some of them are still my closest friends. C Elena, who helped build houses in Peru I'd never used a hammer before, and at first I felt useless next to local builders who could do everything twice as fast. The organisation charged volunteers a large fee, and I later discovered that only a small part of it reached the community, which made me angry. I'd tell anyone thinking of volunteering to research the organisation carefully. Even so, I'm proud of the two houses we finished. We lived with local families and worked six days a week alongside people from the community, who taught us everything with great patience. D Josh, who worked at a children's hospital in Ghana I wasn't a doctor or a nurse, so my job was to play with the children and help their parents with practical things. Some days were very hard emotionally. The thing I'm most grateful for is learning to cope without the comforts I'd always taken for granted, such as hot water and a fast internet connection. I came home much more patient than I was when I left. Many of the children had to stay in hospital for months, so the playroom was the most important place in the building for them. I organised games, drawing and, once, a small concert by the children for their parents.

Should schools set homework? C1 · so ý
Bài

A Dr Helen Marsh, education researcher The research on homework is more consistent than the public debate suggests. For students in secondary school, well-designed homework is clearly linked to higher achievement, but the gains stop at around an hour a night; beyond that, extra work adds nothing except anxiety. For younger children, by contrast, there is simply no reliable evidence of benefit, and I see no reason to keep it in primary schools. What matters most is that teachers actually respond to what students produce. Homework that is collected and never discussed teaches pupils only that it does not matter. Schools that have dropped homework in the early years have seen no fall in later results. B Mark Davies, headteacher I have run schools for twenty years, and I am convinced that homework builds the habits of independent study that pupils need later on. That is why I set short reading and spelling tasks even for six-year-olds: the routine is the point. Pupils who arrive at secondary school without that habit, in my experience, struggle for years. Older students certainly benefit too. I am less persuaded by claims that homework ruins family life; most parents I speak to are glad to see what their children are learning. Detailed marking of every task, frankly, matters less than the habit of regular practice. C Priya Shah, parent and journalist In our house, homework has caused more arguments than anything else. Evenings that could be spent playing, reading for fun or simply sleeping are swallowed by worksheets that my children find pointless. I am not convinced that any of it improves results, at any age; the studies I have read seem weak to me, and they rarely measure what children lose. Where homework is genuinely interesting, such as a small research project, my children throw themselves into it, which tells me that the problem is the kind of task we usually give them. When I raised this at a parents' meeting, I discovered that many other families felt exactly the same. D Professor Leon Brooks, psychologist Practising at home helps students to remember what they have learned in class, particularly when the practice is spread over several days, and the evidence for older students is strong. Short, regular tasks are far more effective than a single long assignment at the weekend. Young children are a different matter; I would set them nothing beyond encouraging them to read for pleasure. Above all, homework is wasted unless teachers give feedback on it, because students cannot correct mistakes they do not know they have made. As for stress at home, it is real, but it usually comes from long, poorly explained tasks.


• Which writer has a different view from the others on whether young children should be given homework? → B Mark Davies (Davies giao bài cả cho trẻ 6 tuổi; Marsh, Shah, Brooks đều không thấy lợi ích ở trẻ nhỏ.)
• Which writer has a different opinion from the others about the academic value of homework for older students? → C Priya Shah (Shah không tin bài về nhà nâng kết quả ở bất kỳ tuổi nào; ba người kia thấy có lợi cho học sinh lớn.)
• Which writer shares A's view on the importance of teachers' feedback? → D Professor Leon Brooks (Brooks: homework is wasted unless teachers give feedback (như Marsh); Davies cho là chấm kỹ ít quan trọng.)
• Which writer suggests that there is a limit beyond which extra homework brings no benefit? → A Dr Helen Marsh (Marsh: the gains stop at around an hour a night.)
Is space exploration worth the cost? C1 · so ý
Bài

A Dr Sarah Lindqvist, astrophysicist Critics who call space exploration a luxury rarely notice how small the budget is: most space agencies receive less than one per cent of what their governments spend each year. For that modest sum, we have gained satellite weather forecasting, better materials and a much deeper understanding of our own planet. I would, however, send robots rather than people wherever possible. They cost a fraction as much, take no risks with human life and return far more data. Every major advance in our understanding of the solar system over the past forty years has come from robotic probes. I welcome private launch companies, which have already cut the cost of reaching orbit. B James Holloway, economist The familiar argument that space programmes produce useful inventions does not stand up. If a society wants better batteries or medical scanners, it is far cheaper to fund that research directly than to hope it emerges as a by-product of a rocket. Supporters of space programmes always point to a handful of famous inventions, but they never count the far larger number of projects that produced nothing useful at all. With so many urgent problems on Earth, from disease to unsafe drinking water, public money should go there first. I have no objection to private firms spending their own investors' money on rockets; that is their business. C Amina Bello, science writer The technologies developed for space have transformed everyday life, from the cameras in our phones to water purification systems. But the true value of crewed missions lies elsewhere: nothing inspires young people to study science like watching a human being step onto another world, and no robot can do that. When I visit schools, the questions children ask are almost always about astronauts, not about probes or telescopes. What worries me is the growing role of private companies, whose commercial interests do not always match the public good and whose satellites are filling orbit with dangerous debris. D Professor Paul Grant, engineer From an engineering point of view, the case for robotic missions is overwhelming: they are cheaper, more reliable and can go where humans never could. A human crew needs food, water, air and a way home; a robot needs only power and a working radio. Human spaceflight is largely about national prestige. Even so, the knowledge gained in designing spacecraft has changed manufacturing, and private companies are now driving exactly the kind of rapid innovation that state agencies struggled to achieve. Satellites monitoring the climate alone justify much of the spending.


• Which writer has a different view from the others on the value of technological by-products of space programmes? → B James Holloway (Holloway: the argument… does not stand up; ba người kia đều cho là có giá trị.)
• Which writer has a different opinion from the others about the role of private companies? → C Amina Bello (Bello lo ngại công ty tư nhân; Lindqvist hoan nghênh, Holloway không phản đối, Grant khen.)
• Which writer shares A's opinion about robotic missions? → D Professor Paul Grant (Grant: the case for robotic missions is overwhelming (như Lindqvist).)
• Which writer points out that space programmes receive only a small share of government spending? → A Dr Sarah Lindqvist (Lindqvist: less than one per cent of what their governments spend.)
Working from home: four views C1 · so ý
Bài

A Rachel Kim, human resources director In our company, output has stayed the same or risen since most staff began working from home several days a week, and people report a much better balance between work and family. We have also been able to recruit people who live too far away to travel to the office every day. The cost falls on younger employees, who no longer pick up skills by overhearing experienced colleagues and who are noticed less by managers. Offices will become smaller, but they will survive as places where teams meet in person. B Dr Omar Farouk, organisational psychologist People do concentrate better at home on tasks that need focus, but creative work suffers when colleagues never meet by chance. Video calls are useful for planned discussions, but they are a poor substitute for the conversations that happen by the coffee machine. Junior staff in particular lose the informal guidance that once came from sitting near experienced colleagues. I also see the boundary between work and private life disappearing: many people now answer messages late into the evening and end up working longer hours than before. In my view the office remains essential and will not shrink as much as many predict. C Tom Reid, owner of a small design business When my team worked entirely from home, productivity in my firm dropped noticeably, and we now meet in the office three days a week. I was sceptical about the whole idea at first, but our experience has persuaded me that a mixed arrangement works best for a small firm like ours. What surprised me was how well our youngest designers did online. With clear systems, regular video meetings and a mentor assigned to each of them, they learned quickly and spoke up more than they ever had in meetings. We gave up half of our office space last year, and I suspect many firms will do the same. My staff say they have more time for their families. D Claire Dubois, economist The productivity figures are mixed, and anyone who claims certainty either way is reading the evidence selectively. Some studies find small gains, others small losses, and much depends on how output is measured in the first place. What is clearer is that demand for office space will fall sharply, with serious consequences for city centres. Younger workers are the group most likely to lose out, because promotion still depends on being seen. And like many researchers, I find that home workers often end up working longer days, as the time once spent travelling is simply absorbed by work.


• Which writer has a different view from the others on how working from home affects young employees? → C Tom Reid (Reid: nhân viên trẻ làm tốt khi làm từ xa; ba người kia cho là họ thiệt.)
• Which writer shares B's view that working from home can lead to longer working hours? → D Claire Dubois (Dubois: home workers often end up working longer days.)
• Which writer has a different opinion from the others about the future size of offices? → B Dr Omar Farouk (Farouk: văn phòng sẽ không thu nhỏ nhiều; ba người kia cho là thu nhỏ.)
• Which writer reports both stable or higher output and a better work-life balance? → A Rachel Kim (Kim: output has stayed the same or risen… a much better balance.)
In Praise of Boredom C1 · bài dài
Bài

There was a time, not so long ago, when waiting for a bus meant staring at the road. Today, the moment a queue forms, almost everyone in it reaches for a phone. We have, it seems, collectively decided that boredom is a problem to be eliminated, and technology has given us the means to eliminate it entirely. I want to suggest that we may have made a poor bargain. Boredom has rarely enjoyed a good reputation. Philosophers have described it as a kind of spiritual emptiness, teachers treat it as a sign that a lesson has failed, and parents rush to fill every gap in their children's timetables. Yet psychologists who have studied the experience in recent years have reached a more interesting conclusion. Boredom, they argue, is not simply the absence of stimulation; it is a signal, much like hunger, telling us that what we are currently doing is not satisfying and that we should look for something better. Seen this way, the trouble with constantly filling empty moments is not that it wastes time, but that it silences the signal before we can act on it. Scrolling through a news feed relieves the discomfort of having nothing to do without ever addressing its cause. We end the day feeling oddly flat, having been occupied for hours without being engaged by anything. There is also evidence that boredom can be productive. In one frequently cited experiment, participants who first spent fifteen minutes on a deliberately dull task, copying numbers from a telephone directory, then produced more original ideas in a creativity test than those who went straight to it. The researchers suggested that a mind left without stimulation begins to wander, and that this wandering allows unexpected connections to form. Many writers and scientists have described their best ideas arriving not at their desks but during long walks, in the shower or on tedious train journeys. I do not want to romanticise the experience. Chronic boredom, the kind experienced by people in repetitive jobs or in institutions with nothing to do, is associated with anxiety, depression and risky behaviour, and nobody would wish it on anyone. Nor is every idle moment secretly creative; often the wandering mind simply returns to the same small worries. The point is not that boredom is good in itself, but that brief, ordinary episodes of it seem to serve a purpose that we interrupt at our cost. Part of the difficulty in following this advice is that modern technology is designed to hold our attention. The apps on our phones are built by teams whose success is measured by how long we stay, and they are extremely good at their jobs. Faced with an endless supply of short, mildly interesting content, a brief spell of boredom has little chance; it is hardly a fair contest, and most of us lose it many times a day. Children, who are given phones at ever younger ages, may have even fewer opportunities to learn what to do with an empty afternoon. What, then, should we do? Banning phones from bus queues is neither possible nor desirable. A more realistic aim might be to notice the impulse to reach for distraction and occasionally to resist it, to let a few minutes pass without input and see what the mind does with them. Some schools have begun to build short periods of unstructured time into the day, partly to give pupils the chance to discover what they actually want to do. It is a modest experiment, but it reflects a growing recognition that a life without empty moments may also be a life with fewer new thoughts.


• What point does the writer make in the first paragraph? → People now avoid boredom almost automatically. (the moment a queue forms, almost everyone in it reaches for a phone.)
• According to psychologists, boredom is best understood as → a message that we should change what we are doing. (a signal… telling us that what we are currently doing is not satisfying.)
• What does the writer say is the real problem with filling every empty moment? → It removes the discomfort without dealing with its cause. (relieves the discomfort… without ever addressing its cause.)
• The experiment with the telephone directory suggests that → a dull activity may help people to think more creatively. (then produced more original ideas in a creativity test.)
• In the fifth paragraph, the writer is careful to point out that → some kinds of boredom are harmful. (Chronic boredom… is associated with anxiety, depression and risky behaviour.)
• What does the writer suggest in the final paragraph? → We should sometimes choose not to fill a quiet moment. (notice the impulse to reach for distraction and occasionally to resist it.)
The Restorer's Dilemma C1 · bài dài
Bài

When I was asked to lead the restoration of a fourteenth-century castle on the Welsh border, I assumed that the hardest decisions would be technical: how to stabilise walls that had been leaning for six hundred years, or how to stop rainwater seeping into the great hall. In fact the most difficult questions turned out to be philosophical. What, exactly, were we restoring the building to? A castle like this one is not a single object frozen at one moment in history. It was extended in the fifteenth century, partly demolished during a civil war, converted into a farmhouse in the eighteenth century and finally abandoned in the nineteenth. Each period left marks on the stone: a blocked medieval window here, a Georgian fireplace there, the scars of cannon fire on the eastern tower. To choose one date and rebuild the castle as it supposedly looked at that time would mean destroying the evidence of every other. The technical work, when it came, was demanding enough. Engineers spent months monitoring the leaning walls with sensors before deciding that they could be held in place with stainless steel ties rather than rebuilt. Ivy, which had been both damaging the stonework and holding parts of it together, had to be removed one section at a time. Every stone that came loose was numbered, recorded and, wherever possible, returned to its original position. Much of this work is invisible to visitors, which is exactly as it should be, and it took almost two years before we could turn to the questions that would prove far harder. There was considerable pressure to do exactly that. Local businesses wanted a building that looked like a castle in a children's book, complete with battlements, which they believed would attract more tourists. Some members of the funding committee asked whether we could rebuild the collapsed north tower. I understood the appeal, but nobody knew what the tower had looked like, and anything we built would have been a guess presented as history. The principle we eventually adopted was one that many conservation professionals now share: repair as much as necessary but as little as possible. Where stone had to be replaced to keep a wall standing, we used material from the same quarry and allowed it to show as new rather than ageing it artificially. Visitors can see clearly which parts are original and which are ours. Where we found the Georgian fireplace blocking a medieval doorway, we left both, with a panel explaining the change. The building, in other words, tells its own story, including the parts of the story that are inconvenient. Not everyone was satisfied. A local newspaper complained that we had spent a great deal of public money on a ruin that still looked like a ruin, and visitor numbers in the first year were lower than the businesses had hoped. I have some sympathy with the disappointment. Ruins are harder to read than complete buildings, and we had perhaps underestimated how much help visitors would need to imagine what had been there. Our answer was not to rebuild but to explain. We added a small exhibition and a free app that shows, as you walk through the rooms, how each space looked at different periods. Visitor numbers have risen steadily since. I am still convinced that we made the right decision. A restored building that pretends to be something it never was may please visitors for a while, but it deceives them, and it destroys the very evidence that later generations, with better methods than ours, might have used to understand it more fully.


• What did the writer discover about the restoration project? → The most difficult questions were not technical ones. (the most difficult questions turned out to be philosophical.)
• Why did the writer reject the idea of rebuilding the castle as it looked at one date? → It would remove signs of other periods in its history. (would mean destroying the evidence of every other.)
• What was the writer's objection to rebuilding the north tower? → Its original design was unknown. (nobody knew what the tower had looked like… a guess presented as history.)
• How did the team treat new stone used in repairs? → They made no attempt to make it look old. (allowed it to show as new rather than ageing it artificially.)
• How does the writer respond to the newspaper's criticism? → He accepts that visitors needed more help to understand the site. (we had perhaps underestimated how much help visitors would need.)
• What is the writer's main argument in the final paragraph? → False restoration misleads people and destroys evidence. (it deceives them, and it destroys the very evidence…)
Where Ideas Come From: Four Artists C1 · nối
Bài

A Clara Voss, painter I distrust the idea of inspiration arriving like a flash of light. My best work comes from routine: I am in the studio at eight every morning, whether or not I feel like painting, and most of what I produce on any given day is unremarkable. Occasionally, though, something unplanned happens on the canvas, and I have learned to recognise those moments and follow them. Young artists often ask me for the secret, and they are visibly disappointed when I tell them it is mostly showing up. I keep a box of failed paintings under the stairs; looking through it reminds me how many wrong turns it takes to find a right one. I also rarely show unfinished work to anyone, not even my family, because early opinions tend to push a painting in a direction it was never meant to take. B Daniel Achebe, composer Much of my music begins with sounds I hear by accident: a lorry reversing, rain on a metal roof, two conversations overlapping on a train. I record them on my phone and listen back weeks later, when I have forgotten where they came from. Collaboration is essential to me; I rarely write anything without first discussing it with the musicians who will perform it, and their suggestions often change a piece completely. I find working alone for long periods quite unhealthy, both for the music and for me. Some of the pieces I am proudest of were written for amateur choirs, whose enthusiasm more than makes up for their limited technique, and who are often braver than professionals. C Mira Sato, novelist Ideas are never my problem; I have notebooks full of them. The difficulty is deciding which ones deserve two or three years of my life. My test is simple: if a character is still on my mind after six months, I start writing. I plan very little in advance and often have no idea how a novel will end until I reach the final chapters, which my publisher finds alarming. Reading outside my own field, especially history and science, feeds my imagination far more than reading other novelists. I write every morning for four hours and spend the afternoons walking, which is usually when problems in the plot quietly solve themselves without my noticing. D Paolo Rinaldi, choreographer I begin with the dancers rather than with an idea. In the first weeks of rehearsal I simply watch how each of them moves and ask them to improvise, and the piece grows out of what I see. For that reason, no two performances of my work are identical, and I like it that way. Criticism used to wound me deeply, but I have come to see a bad review as information rather than judgement. The worst thing an artist can do, I think, is repeat a past success because audiences expect it. Our company tours small towns as well as major theatres, and some of my favourite performances have taken place in school halls, in front of people who had never seen dance before.

• Which artist often uses sounds from everyday life? → B Daniel Achebe (Achebe: Much of my music begins with sounds I hear by accident.)
• Which artist says that they have more ideas than they could use? → C Mira Sato (Sato: Ideas are never my problem; I have notebooks full of them.)
• Which artist builds new work from what performers do? → D Paolo Rinaldi (Rinaldi: the piece grows out of what I see.)
• Which artist finds that ideas from other subjects are especially useful? → C Mira Sato (Sato: Reading outside my own field… feeds my imagination.)
• Which artist has changed the way they react to negative comments? → D Paolo Rinaldi (Rinaldi: I have come to see a bad review as information rather than judgement.)
• Which artist says that others are disappointed by the advice they give? → A Clara Voss (Voss: they are visibly disappointed when I tell them…)
