• Which artist warns against copying earlier successful work? → D Paolo Rinaldi (Rinaldi: The worst thing… is repeat a past success.)
The Museum of the Future: Four Curators C1 · nối
Bài

A Hannah Weiss, director of a science museum Twenty years ago, a museum visit meant walking past glass cases and reading small labels. Now visitors expect to touch, try and take part, and we have redesigned almost every gallery around activities. The change has worked: families stay twice as long as they used to. My concern is that we may have gone too far, and that some of the quieter pleasures of looking closely at a real object are being lost. We are now experimenting with one gallery where nothing at all is interactive. Schools remain our most important visitors, and every new exhibition is now designed together with teachers, who tell us very quickly what children will ignore and what will hold their attention. B Kofi Mensah, curator of African art Museums like mine hold thousands of objects that were taken from their countries of origin during the colonial period, often by force. I believe we have a duty to return those that communities ask for, and we have already sent several collections home. Some colleagues fear that this will empty our galleries, but in practice returns have led to new partnerships, loans and exhibitions that we could never have created alone. The museum of the future will be a meeting place, not a storehouse. Each return involves long discussions with community leaders, historians and governments, and these conversations have taught my staff a great deal about the objects themselves and the people who made them. C Lucía Ortega, head of digital collections Only about five per cent of our collection is ever on display; the rest sits in storage. Photographing and publishing the whole collection online has opened it to researchers and the public across the world, and our online visitors now outnumber those who come through the doors. People sometimes ask whether this will make physical visits unnecessary. I don't think so: in our experience, seeing an object online makes people more, not less, likely to want to see it in person. The project was slower and more costly than we expected, mainly because every object had to be checked, measured and described by a specialist before it could be photographed. D James Whitfield, director of a small local museum Much of the debate about the future of museums is about large national institutions with large budgets. In a small town, our challenge is simply survival. We depend almost entirely on volunteers, and our income comes from the café and a few events each year. What keeps us going is the local community, who bring us objects, photographs and memories. I am wary of expensive technology; a good conversation with a knowledgeable volunteer often means more to visitors than any screen. Last year we held an exhibition of wedding photographs lent by local families, and it attracted more visitors than anything we had ever done before, many of whom had never set foot in the museum.


• Which curator thinks that a recent change may have had an unwanted effect? → A Hannah Weiss (Weiss: we may have gone too far… quieter pleasures… are being lost.)
• Which curator mentions that colleagues are worried about losing objects? → B Kofi Mensah (Mensah: Some colleagues fear that this will empty our galleries.)
• Which curator says that most of the collection cannot normally be seen? → C Lucía Ortega (Ortega: Only about five per cent… is ever on display.)
• Which curator relies mainly on unpaid helpers? → D James Whitfield (Whitfield: We depend almost entirely on volunteers.)
• Which curator reports that visitors now spend longer in the museum? → A Hannah Weiss (Weiss: families stay twice as long.)
• Which curator believes that giving objects back has created new opportunities? → B Kofi Mensah (Mensah: returns have led to new partnerships, loans and exhibitions.)
• Which curator argues that seeing things online encourages real visits? → C Lucía Ortega (Ortega: seeing an object online makes people more… likely to want to see it in person.)
• Which curator is cautious about spending money on new technology? → D James Whitfield (Whitfield: I am wary of expensive technology.)
• Which curator is testing an approach that goes against a recent trend? → A Hannah Weiss (Weiss: one gallery where nothing at all is interactive.)
• Which curator feels that the debate ignores institutions like theirs? → D James Whitfield (Whitfield: Much of the debate… is about large national institutions.)
On Forgetting C2 · bài dài
Bài

We tend to speak of forgetting as a failure, a small betrayal by a brain that cannot be trusted to hold on to what matters. We apologise for forgetting names, worry when we cannot recall why we walked into a room, and spend considerable sums on apps and courses that promise to sharpen our memories. Rarely do we stop to ask whether forgetting might be doing something useful, or whether a mind that retained everything would be any better off. There is, in fact, a famous case that suggests it would not. In the early twentieth century, a Russian journalist came to the attention of the psychologist Alexander Luria because he appeared to remember everything. He could reproduce long tables of numbers years after seeing them once, and recall the exact words of conversations he had barely attended to. Yet his extraordinary memory was far from a blessing. He struggled to follow the meaning of a story because every word called up a cascade of images, and he found it difficult to recognise faces, which change from day to day, because he remembered each version too precisely to see what they had in common. To understand, it seems, we must be able to let details go. This insight lies at the heart of a growing body of research which treats forgetting not as decay but as an active process. Some neuroscientists now argue that the brain spends considerable energy weakening the connections that hold memories in place, and that it does so selectively. Information that is rarely used fades; information that is repeatedly retrieved grows stronger. The result is a memory tuned less to the past than to the future, preserving what has proved useful and discarding what has not. Forgetting also appears to help us generalise. A child who has been bitten by one particular dog needs to remember that dogs can bite, not every detail of that afternoon. By losing the specific and keeping the general, the mind turns experience into knowledge that can be applied in new situations. Researchers who build artificial learning systems have observed something similar: programs that memorise their training examples too faithfully often perform badly when faced with anything unfamiliar. None of this means that every lapse is beneficial. The forgetting associated with illnesses such as dementia is a genuine loss, and the frustration of losing a word in mid-sentence is real enough. Nor does the research suggest that we should stop trying to learn. The point is rather that memory and forgetting work together, and that a system designed to remember everything would be less, not more, intelligent. There may be a personal dimension, too. People who cannot let go of painful memories, who replay old humiliations and quarrels in vivid detail, often suffer for it. Psychologists have long observed that the ability to allow certain experiences to lose their sharpness is part of recovering from them. Forgiveness, in this sense, may owe something to forgetting, or at least to a softening of the edges that time and a healthy brain provide. Cultures seem to have understood this long before the neuroscience. Oral traditions, which passed stories from one generation to the next without writing, never preserved every detail of the original telling; each storyteller kept the shape and the meaning of a tale while freely changing its surface. Far from being a weakness, this flexibility allowed stories to survive for centuries, adapting to new audiences while keeping what mattered. Written records, by contrast, preserve everything equally, the trivial alongside the essential, and leave it to the reader to decide which is which. Historians of the distant past often complain that they have too little evidence; those who study the recent past increasingly complain that they have far too much, and no time to read it. It is worth remembering, then, that the next time a name escapes us, our brains may simply be doing their job. In a world that offers us more information each day than our grandparents encountered in a year, the capacity to discard may be as valuable as the capacity to store. We have built machines that remember everything; perhaps the more human achievement is knowing what to forget.


• In the first paragraph, the writer suggests that people → rarely consider whether forgetting has any value. (Rarely do we stop to ask whether forgetting might be doing something useful.)
• The case of the Russian journalist is mentioned to show that → a perfect memory can prevent understanding. (To understand, it seems, we must be able to let details go.)
• According to some neuroscientists, the brain → deliberately weakens memories that are seldom used. (spends considerable energy weakening the connections… selectively.)
• Why does the writer mention artificial learning systems? → to show a parallel with the way human memory generalises (Researchers who build artificial learning systems have observed something similar.)
• What does the writer say about forgetting in the fifth paragraph? → Some forms of it are clearly harmful. (The forgetting associated with illnesses such as dementia is a genuine loss.)
• In the final paragraph, the writer concludes that → the ability to forget is especially valuable today. (the capacity to discard may be as valuable as the capacity to store.)
The Translator's Invisible Art C2 · bài dài
Bài

When a translation is praised, the praise is usually for the author. Reviewers admire the elegance of the prose or the precision of the dialogue, and only occasionally remember that every word they are quoting was chosen not by the writer but by me. I do not resent this; in many ways it is the highest compliment a translator can receive. If readers forget that the book was written in another language, I have done my job. Yet the invisibility of the work hides how many decisions it involves, and how few of them have a right answer. Consider something as apparently simple as a joke. The novel I am currently translating, from Portuguese, contains a scene in which two characters exchange insults based on a pun that cannot exist in English. I can translate the words literally, in which case the scene makes no sense; I can add a footnote explaining the pun, which kills the humour as effectively as explaining any joke; or I can invent a new pun in English that does a similar job. I have chosen the third option, which means that a few lines of the English novel are, strictly speaking, mine rather than the author's. Some translators would consider this a betrayal. I consider it the only form of faithfulness available. The problem extends far beyond wordplay. Languages divide the world differently. Portuguese has a word, saudade, for a kind of longing for something absent, perhaps lost for ever, that English can only approach through a phrase. Forms of address carry social information that has no English equivalent; whether a character uses a formal or informal 'you' to another can tell a Portuguese reader everything about their relationship, while an English reader notices nothing. The translator must decide, again and again, whether to preserve the strangeness of the original or to smooth it into something that feels natural. There is no consensus on how to make that choice. One tradition holds that a translation should read as if it had been written in the target language, so that readers are never reminded of the original. Another insists that a good translation should feel slightly foreign, reminding readers that they are encountering a different culture rather than a version of their own. I find myself moving between the two, sometimes within a single page. Dialogue usually needs to sound natural, because people do not speak in translation; descriptions of a city or a meal can afford to keep a little of their original flavour. I read every chapter aloud before I send it to the publisher. A sentence can be accurate in every word and still be wrong, because its rhythm stumbles where the original flowed, or because it is half as long as the sentence it replaces and so loses the breathlessness the author intended. Reading aloud exposes these problems in a way that silent reading rarely does, and it is one of the reasons why a chapter that takes me a week to draft may take another week to revise. My partner, who has heard more Portuguese novels in English than anyone should, claims she can tell from the next room whether a chapter is finished. The working conditions of translators rarely match the importance of these decisions. Most of us are paid by the word, at rates that have barely changed in twenty years, and our names are often missing from the cover. Publishers in the English-speaking world translate remarkably few books, partly because they assume readers are reluctant to buy them. The success of several translated novels in recent years suggests that this assumption may be wrong, and there are signs that attitudes are changing, but slowly. I am sometimes asked whether machines will soon do my job. Automatic translation has become impressively accurate for instructions, contracts and technical documents, and I use it myself to check my first drafts. But a novel is not a set of instructions. Its meaning lies as much in rhythm, implication and silence as in the dictionary sense of its words, and these are precisely the things a machine handles worst. A program can tell me what a sentence says; it cannot yet tell me what the sentence is doing, or what it would cost to lose.


• How does the writer feel about reviewers praising the author rather than the translator? → She sees it as a sign of successful work. (in many ways it is the highest compliment a translator can receive.)
• What does the writer say about her approach to the pun? → She believes changing the words keeps the author's intention. (I consider it the only form of faithfulness available.)
• The example of formal and informal 'you' illustrates → information that may be lost in translation. (can tell a Portuguese reader everything… while an English reader notices nothing.)
• How does the writer deal with the two traditions of translation? → She uses each one depending on the type of passage. (I find myself moving between the two… Dialogue usually needs to sound natural.)
• What does the writer suggest about English-language publishers? → Their view of readers may be mistaken. (suggests that this assumption may be wrong.)
• What is the writer's view of machine translation? → It cannot yet deal with what is most important in literature. (these are precisely the things a machine handles worst.)
First Novels: Four Writers Look Back C2 · nối
Bài

A Eleanor Price My first novel was rejected by eleven publishers, and in retrospect most of them were right to reject it. It was far too long and full of beautiful sentences that did nothing for the story. The twelfth publisher accepted it on condition that I cut a third of it, and I spent a miserable summer doing so. It was the most useful education I have ever had. When I reread the book today I can still see the scars of that editing, but I also see a writer learning, painfully, to trust the reader. I sometimes give the original manuscript to students as an example of what not to do, which they find enormously reassuring. The book eventually found a small but loyal readership, mostly through public libraries, and it is still in print more than twenty years later, which nobody, least of all the eleven publishers, predicted. B Samuel Okonjo I wrote my first novel in the evenings while working as a hospital porter, and I had no idea whether it was any good, because I showed it to nobody. That secrecy was a mistake. When it was finally published, a reviewer pointed out a flaw in the plot so obvious that I could not believe I had missed it, and any friend could have spotted it in an afternoon. The book sold modestly, but it was translated into several languages, which surprised me, and a reader in Brazil still writes to me every year. I now ask at least three people to read every draft. Night shifts, oddly, were ideal for writing: the corridors were quiet after two in the morning, and I filled notebook after notebook between calls to move patients from one ward to another. C Ingrid Holm I was thirty-eight when my first novel came out, and I had spent fifteen years writing short stories that no one wanted. People assume that the novel was my breakthrough, but in fact it was those years of failure that made it possible; every technique I used had been tested, and usually spoiled, in a story first. The book did unexpectedly well, which brought its own problems. For two years I could not write anything, because every sentence seemed to be competing with the success of the first book. I would advise new writers to start the next book before the first one is published. The short stories that nobody wanted at the time have since been collected and published, and some readers now tell me, rather to my embarrassment, that they prefer them to the novels. D Rafael Moreno My first novel was largely autobiographical, which is common, and which caused trouble I had not anticipated. Members of my family recognised themselves in characters I had believed were thoroughly disguised, and one of my brothers did not speak to me for a year. I do not regret writing it, but I would handle it differently now. The experience also taught me that readers are much less interested in whether a story really happened than writers imagine; what matters to them is whether it feels true. My later books, which are entirely invented, have been praised for their honesty, which I find rather amusing. I did show the manuscript to my mother before it was published, and she said nothing at all about it, which I mistakenly took as a sign of approval rather than of shock.


• Which writer says that a long period without success prepared them for writing the novel? → C Ingrid Holm (Holm: it was those years of failure that made it possible.)
• Which writer regrets not asking others for their opinion before publication? → B Samuel Okonjo (Okonjo: I showed it to nobody. That secrecy was a mistake.)
• Which writer had to make the book much shorter? → A Eleanor Price (Price: cut a third of it.)
• Which writer upset people who appeared in the book? → D Rafael Moreno (Moreno: one of my brothers did not speak to me for a year.)
• Which writer found that success made further writing difficult? → C Ingrid Holm (Holm: For two years I could not write anything.)
• Which writer uses the first book to encourage people who are learning to write? → A Eleanor Price (Price: I sometimes give the original manuscript to students.)
• Which writer was surprised that the book was read abroad? → B Samuel Okonjo (Okonjo: translated into several languages, which surprised me.)
• Which writer finds it ironic how their later work has been described? → D Rafael Moreno (Moreno: My later books, which are entirely invented, have been praised for their honesty.)
• Which writer wrote the book alongside another job? → B Samuel Okonjo (Okonjo: while working as a hospital porter.)
• Which writer agrees with the publishers who turned down the book? → A Eleanor Price (Price: most of them were right to reject it.)
Four Mountaineers Remember C2 · nối
Bài

A Thomas Berg The climb people ask me about is the one that made my name, but the expedition I think about most is one we abandoned four hundred metres below the summit. The weather was turning, and one of our team was showing early signs of altitude sickness. Turning back was the hardest decision I have ever made, and for months afterwards I wondered whether I had been a coward. I now believe it was the best decision of my career. Two members of another team who continued that day did not come home. Since then I have measured a successful expedition by whether everyone returns, not by whether we reach the top. These days I spend more time teaching young climbers to read the weather than climbing high peaks myself. If I can pass on one lesson, it is that the mountain will still be there next year. B Aiko Tanaka I came to climbing late, at thirty-four, after a career in banking, and I have never quite lost the sense that I am an outsider in the climbing world. That has its advantages. I approach each expedition like a business project, with detailed budgets and contingency plans, and some of the more romantic climbers I have worked with found this faintly ridiculous until it saved them. On one expedition our supplies were delayed for a week, and the only reason we could continue was that I had paid for a reserve stock in advance. My former colleagues assumed I had given up a secure life for a reckless hobby. In truth, I take fewer risks on a mountain than I ever took with other people's money, because the consequences of a mistake are so immediate. C Grace O'Neill What I remember most vividly is not any summit but the silence. On one long climb in Alaska we spent nine days on a single face, and for much of that time the only sounds were our breathing and the wind. I had expected to find the isolation frightening; instead, it was the most peaceful I have ever felt. Coming home was harder than the climb itself. The noise and speed of ordinary life overwhelmed me for weeks, and I am not sure the people around me ever understood why. Since then I have climbed in smaller, quieter places rather than chasing famous summits, and I have written two books about solitude in the mountains, which to my publisher's surprise have sold rather well. D Viktor Lenz I have climbed with the same partner for twenty-two years, and I would not attempt a serious route with anyone else. On a mountain you have to know, without speaking, what the other person will do, and that kind of understanding takes years to build. I worry about the commercial expeditions that now take paying clients to the highest peaks. Many of those clients have never climbed together before, and some have barely climbed at all, and they depend entirely on guides who cannot possibly watch everyone at once. My partner and I have turned back from many routes together without a word of argument, because each of us trusts the other's judgement completely. That kind of trust is worth more than any equipment you can buy. Younger climbers sometimes ask me how to find a partner like mine, and I tell them to start on small routes and to be very patient.


• Which climber judges success differently because of a past experience? → A Thomas Berg (Berg: Since then I have measured a successful expedition by whether everyone returns.)
• Which climber found returning to normal life difficult? → C Grace O'Neill (O'Neill: Coming home was harder than the climb itself.)
• Which climber's careful planning rescued an expedition? → B Aiko Tanaka (Tanaka: the only reason we could continue was that I had paid for a reserve stock in advance.)
• Which climber expresses concern about people who pay to be taken up mountains? → D Viktor Lenz (Lenz: I worry about the commercial expeditions…)
• Which climber doubted their own courage for a while? → A Thomas Berg (Berg: I wondered whether I had been a coward.)
• Which climber was surprised by their own reaction to being alone? → C Grace O'Neill (O'Neill: I had expected to find the isolation frightening; instead, it was the most peaceful…)
• Which climber started climbing after a different career? → B Aiko Tanaka (Tanaka: after a career in banking.)
• Which climber values a long partnership above all? → D Viktor Lenz (Lenz: the same partner for twenty-two years.)
• Which climber says other climbers once laughed at their methods? → B Aiko Tanaka (Tanaka: found this faintly ridiculous until it saved them.)
• Which climber feels that others did not understand their experience? → C Grace O'Neill (O'Neill: I am not sure the people around me ever understood why.)

🧱 Ngữ pháp Nền · 12 điểm mới (v124) (12)

Điểm còn thiếu so với khung A1 → B2 (gợi ý, đại từ phản thân, -ed / -ing, tính từ + giới từ, for / since, câu hỏi chủ ngữ, can / could / be able to, mệnh đề thời gian tương lai, make / let / allow, both / either / neither, may / might / could, quá khứ hoàn thành tiếp diễn). Kiểm: công thức, cách dùng đúng; mỗi câu chỉ một đáp án; câu "sai" thật sự sai.

Rủ rê, gợi ý: let's, shall we, why don't we, how about A1
Let's + động từ nguyên mẫu: Let's go to the park.
Shall we + V? · Why don't we + V? · How about + V-ing?
Rủ người khác cùng làm gì. · Trả lời: Good idea! / OK. / Sorry, I can't. · Sau How about là V-ing: “How about go” là sai → How about going.
• It's hot. Let's ___ swimming. → go (✗ going · ✗ to go)
• How about ___ a taxi? → taking (✗ take · ✗ to take)
• ___ we play tennis on Saturday? → Shall (✗ Let's · ✗ How about)
• Why don't we ___ a cake for Mum? → make (✗ making · ✗ to make)
• Let's ___ (eat) outside today. → eat
• How about ___ (visit) Grandma? → visiting
• Shall we ___ (meet) after school? → meet
• ❌ Let's to go home. → Let's go home. (Let's + V, không có to.)
• ❌ How about play football? → How about playing football? (How about + V-ing.)
• ❌ Why don't we going to the beach? → Why don't we go to the beach? (Why don't we + V nguyên mẫu.)
• Let's go to the cinema. ❓ Người nói muốn gì? → Rủ đi xem phim (✗ Kể đã xem phim · ✗ Hỏi giờ chiếu phim)
• How about having lunch at home? ❓ Người nói gợi ý gì? → Ăn trưa ở nhà (✗ Ăn trưa ở ngoài · ✗ Không ăn trưa)
Đại từ phản thân: myself, yourself… A2
myself · yourself · himself · herself · itself · ourselves · yourselves · themselves
Chủ ngữ và tân ngữ là cùng một người: She hurt herself.
Làm gì cho chính mình: I made myself a sandwich. · by + myself = một mình, không ai giúp: I did it by myself. · Không dùng phản thân sau feel / relax / meet thông thường: “I feel myself tired” là sai.
• I taught ___ to play the guitar. → myself (✗ me · ✗ mine)
• The children enjoyed ___ at the zoo. → themselves (✗ them · ✗ themself)
• She lives by ___ in a small flat. → herself (✗ her · ✗ hers)
• Did you make this cake ___? → yourself (✗ you · ✗ your)
• He hurt ___ (he) playing football. → himself
• We cooked dinner by ___ (we). → ourselves
• Look at ___ (you) in the mirror! → yourself / yourselves
• ❌ She looked at her in the mirror. → She looked at herself in the mirror. (Chủ ngữ tự nhìn chính mình → herself.)
• ❌ They built the house by theirself. → They built the house by themselves. (Không có theirself; số nhiều: themselves.)
• ❌ I cut me while I was cooking. → I cut myself while I was cooking. (Tự làm mình bị thương → myself.)
• Tom made himself a cup of tea. ❓ Trà là cho ai? → Cho Tom (✗ Cho bạn của Tom · ✗ Cho mọi người)
• Lan painted the room by herself. ❓ Ai giúp Lan sơn phòng? → Không ai giúp (✗ Bạn của Lan · ✗ Cả nhà Lan)
Tính từ đuôi -ed và -ing A2
-ed: cảm giác của người: bored, interested, tired, excited, surprised
-ing: tính chất của vật / việc gây ra cảm giác: boring, interesting, tiring, exciting, surprising
I'm bored. = Tôi thấy chán. · The film is boring. = Bộ phim chán (làm người xem chán). · “I'm boring” nghĩa là tôi là người nhàm chán, không phải tôi đang chán.
• The lesson was so ___ that I fell asleep. → boring (✗ bored · ✗ bore)
• I'm really ___ about my holiday next week. → excited (✗ exciting · ✗ excite)
• After the long walk we were very ___. → tired (✗ tiring · ✗ tire)
• It was an ___ story. Everyone listened carefully. → interesting (✗ interested · ✗ interest)
• The football match was really ___ (excite). → exciting
• Are you ___ (interest) in history? → interested
• My job is very ___ (tire). I work twelve hours a day. → tiring
• ❌ I'm very interesting in music. → I'm very interested in music. (Cảm giác của người → interested.)
• ❌ The film was bored. → The film was boring. (Bộ phim gây chán → boring.)
• ❌ We were very exciting about the trip. → We were very excited about the trip. (Cảm giác của người → excited.)
• Minh is boring. ❓ Câu này nói gì về Minh? → Minh là người nhàm chán (✗ Minh đang thấy chán · ✗ Minh rất mệt)
• The children were surprised. ❓ Ai ngạc nhiên? → Bọn trẻ (✗ Người kể chuyện · ✗ Không ai cả)
Tính từ + giới từ A2
good at · bad at · interested in · afraid of · proud of · tired of · famous for · different from · worried about · angry with (người) / about (việc)
Sau giới từ là danh từ hoặc V-ing: good at swimming.
Mỗi tính từ đi với giới từ cố định; học theo cụm. · Sau giới từ: V-ing, không phải to V. · “interested about”, “afraid from” là sai → interested in, afraid of.
• My brother is very good ___ drawing. → at (✗ in · ✗ on)
• Are you interested ___ joining our club? → in (✗ about · ✗ for)
• This town is famous ___ its cheese. → for (✗ of · ✗ with)
• I'm worried ___ my exam tomorrow. → about (✗ for · ✗ with)
• My little sister is afraid ___ the dark. → of
• She's proud ___ her son. → of
• I'm tired ___ eating the same food every day. → of
• ❌ He is good in football. → He is good at football. (good at.)
• ❌ I'm interested about learning Japanese. → I'm interested in learning Japanese. (interested in.)
• ❌ She is afraid from dogs. → She is afraid of dogs. (afraid of.)
• Hoa is good at singing but bad at dancing. ❓ Hoa làm giỏi việc gì? → Hát (✗ Nhảy · ✗ Cả hai)
• Nam is tired of his job. ❓ Nam cảm thấy thế nào về công việc? → Chán công việc (✗ Thích công việc · ✗ Sợ công việc)
Hiện tại hoàn thành với for và since A2
have / has + V3 + for + khoảng thời gian: for two years, for a week
have / has + V3 + since + mốc thời gian: since 2020, since Monday
Việc bắt đầu trong quá khứ và vẫn còn tới bây giờ: I have lived here for five years. · Hỏi: How long have you…? · Người Việt hay dùng hiện tại đơn: “I live here since 2020” là sai → I have lived here since 2020.
• We have lived in Hanoi ___ 2015. → since (✗ for · ✗ from)
• He has been a teacher ___ twenty years. → for (✗ since · ✗ during)
• I ___ this phone for three years. → have had (✗ have · ✗ had)
• How long ___ you known your best friend? → have (✗ do · ✗ did)
• She ___ (be) ill since Monday. → has been / 's been
• They ___ (know) each other for a long time. → have known / 've known
• I ___ (not see) him since last summer. → haven't seen / have not seen
• ❌ I live here since 2019. → I have lived here since 2019. (since + mốc, việc còn tới giờ → hiện tại hoàn thành.)
• ❌ She has worked here since three years. → She has worked here for three years. (three years là khoảng → for.)
• ❌ We know each other for ten years. → We have known each other for ten years. (Việc còn tới giờ → have known.)
• I have worked here for two years. ❓ Người nói còn làm ở đây không? → Còn (✗ Không còn · ✗ Không rõ)
• I worked here for two years. ❓ Người nói còn làm ở đây không? → Không còn (✗ Còn · ✗ Mới bắt đầu)
Câu hỏi chủ ngữ và câu hỏi tân ngữ A2
Hỏi chủ ngữ (ai / cái gì LÀM việc đó): Who called you? (không có do / did)
Hỏi tân ngữ (ai / cái gì BỊ tác động): Who did you call? (có do / did)
Who / What đóng vai chủ ngữ → giữ trật tự câu khẳng định: What happened? · Who / What là tân ngữ → dùng trợ động từ như câu hỏi thường. · “What did happen?” là sai khi hỏi chủ ngữ → What happened?
• Who ___ this cake? It's delicious! → made (✗ did make · ✗ did you make it)
• What ___ at the meeting yesterday? → happened (✗ did happen · ✗ did it happen)
• Who ___ you see at the station? → did (✗ was · ✗ —)
• Who ___ to the party? — Everyone in my class. → came (✗ did come · ✗ did you come)
• Who ___ (win) the match last night? → won
• What ___ (you / buy) at the market? → did you buy
• Who ___ (live) in that big house? → lives
• ❌ Who did break the vase? → Who broke the vase? (Hỏi người làm (chủ ngữ): không dùng did.)
• ❌ What did happen to your phone? → What happened to your phone? (What là chủ ngữ → What happened?)
• ❌ Who you called last night? → Who did you call last night? (Hỏi tân ngữ → cần did.)
• Who did Lan help? ❓ Câu hỏi muốn biết điều gì? → Người được Lan giúp (✗ Người đã giúp Lan · ✗ Lúc Lan giúp)
• Who helped Lan? ❓ Câu hỏi muốn biết điều gì? → Người đã giúp Lan (✗ Người được Lan giúp · ✗ Lan giúp ở đâu)
Khả năng: can, could, be able to A2
Hiện tại: can / be able to · Quá khứ: could / was able to
Tương lai và sau động từ khác: will be able to, want to be able to
could: khả năng chung trong quá khứ (I could swim when I was five). · was / were able to: làm được một việc cụ thể (dù khó): I was able to find the key. · Không nói “will can” hay “to can” → will be able to.
• After three months of lessons, I'll ___ drive. → be able to (✗ can · ✗ could)
• When I was young, I ___ run very fast. → could (✗ can · ✗ will be able to)
• The door was locked, but we ___ get in through the window. → were able to (✗ could to · ✗ can)
• I'd like to ___ play the piano. → be able to (✗ can · ✗ could)
• She ___ (can, phủ định) swim until she was ten. → couldn't / could not
• Will you ___ (be able) help me tomorrow? → be able to
• Last night I ___ (be able, phủ định) sleep because of the noise. → wasn't able to / was not able to
• ❌ I will can visit you next week. → I will be able to visit you next week. (Không nói will can → will be able to.)
• ❌ I want to can speak French. → I want to be able to speak French. (Sau to dùng be able to.)
• ❌ Yesterday I can finish my homework early. → Yesterday I was able to finish my homework early. (Quá khứ → was able to / could.)
• I could swim when I was six. ❓ Khi nào người nói biết bơi? → Từ lúc sáu tuổi (✗ Mới học gần đây · ✗ Chưa biết bơi)
• I'll be able to drive next year. ❓ Bây giờ người nói lái xe được chưa? → Chưa (✗ Được rồi · ✗ Không bao giờ)
Mệnh đề thời gian chỉ tương lai B1
when / as soon as / until / before / after + hiện tại đơn, mệnh đề chính dùng will / be going to / mệnh lệnh
I'll call you when I arrive. (không nói when I will arrive)
Nói về việc sẽ xảy ra trong tương lai, nhưng sau từ chỉ thời gian vẫn dùng hiện tại đơn. · Có thể dùng hiện tại hoàn thành để nhấn mạnh việc làm xong: after I've finished. · Lỗi hay gặp: “as soon as I will get home” → as soon as I get home.
• I'll phone you when I ___ to Paris. → get (✗ will get · ✗ got)
• Don't leave until the teacher ___ you. → tells (✗ will tell · ✗ told)
• As soon as the rain ___, we'll go out. → stops (✗ will stop · ✗ stopped)
• She'll start her new job after she ___ university. → finishes (✗ will finish · ✗ would finish)
• I'll lend you the book when I ___ (finish) it. → finish / have finished / 've finished
• Call me before you ___ (leave). → leave
• We won't start the meeting until everyone ___ (arrive). → arrives / has arrived
• ❌ I'll tell her when she will come. → I'll tell her when she comes. (Sau when (tương lai) dùng hiện tại đơn.)
• ❌ As soon as I will arrive, I'll call you. → As soon as I arrive, I'll call you. (Sau as soon as dùng hiện tại đơn.)
• ❌ Wait until the light will turn green. → Wait until the light turns green. (Sau until dùng hiện tại đơn.)
make, let, allow, be allowed to B1
make + người + V nguyên mẫu (bắt): My mum made me tidy my room.
let + người + V nguyên mẫu (cho phép) · allow + người + to V · be allowed to + V
make = bắt buộc; let / allow = cho phép. · Bị động: be made to + V (I was made to wait), be allowed to + V. · “let me to go”, “made me to do” là sai; nhưng “allow me to go” thì đúng.
• My boss made me ___ until nine o'clock. → work (✗ to work · ✗ working)
• Will your parents let you ___ to the concert? → go (✗ to go · ✗ going)
• Students are not ___ to use phones during the exam. → allowed (✗ let · ✗ made)
• The new manager doesn't allow staff ___ long breaks. → to take (✗ take · ✗ taking)
• The film made me ___ (cry). → cry
• We weren't ___ (allow) to take photos inside. → allowed
• She let her little brother ___ (play) with her phone. → play
• ❌ My father let me to drive his car. → My father let me drive his car. (let + người + V nguyên mẫu.)
• ❌ They made us to wear uniforms. → They made us wear uniforms. (make + người + V nguyên mẫu.)
• ❌ Visitors are not let to feed the animals. → Visitors are not allowed to feed the animals. (Bị động của cho phép: be allowed to.)
both, either, neither B1
both (cả hai) + danh từ số nhiều · both … and …
either (một trong hai) / neither (không cái nào trong hai) + danh từ số ít · either … or … · neither … nor …
both of / either of / neither of + the / my / them + số nhiều. · Neither đã mang nghĩa phủ định: không thêm not. · “Neither of them didn't come” là sai (phủ định hai lần) → Neither of them came.
• ___ of my parents are teachers. → Both (✗ Either · ✗ Neither)
• I don't mind. We can go ___ today or tomorrow. → either (✗ both · ✗ neither)
• ___ of the two hotels had a free room, so we slept in the car. → Neither (✗ Both · ✗ Either)
• She speaks ___ French and German. → both (✗ either · ✗ neither)
• ___ of us wanted to cook, so we ordered pizza. → Neither
• You can pay ___ by card or in cash. → either
• ___ my brother and I like football. → Both
• ❌ Neither of them didn't know the answer. → Neither of them knew the answer. (Neither đã phủ định, không thêm didn't.)
• ❌ Both of the answer are wrong. → Both of the answers are wrong. (both of + danh từ số nhiều.)
• ❌ You can choose either the red or blue one or. → You can choose either the red one or the blue one. (Cấu trúc either … or …)
may, might, could: khả năng trong tương lai B1
may / might / could + V nguyên mẫu: It might rain later.
Phủ định: may not / might not (không dùng could not với nghĩa này)
Nói điều có thể xảy ra nhưng không chắc. · might thường ít chắc hơn may một chút; trong giao tiếp gần như tương đương. · “It might rains”, “might to come” là sai → might rain, might come.
• Take a coat. It ___ get cold this evening. → might (✗ must · ✗ should to)
• I'm not sure, but I may ___ late tomorrow. → be (✗ to be · ✗ being)
• He ___ not pass the exam. He hasn't studied much. → might (✗ could · ✗ can)
• We ___ go camping next month if the weather is good. → may (✗ must · ✗ are)
• I'm not sure where Tom is. He ___ (might, be) at the gym. → might be
• Don't wait for me. I ___ (may, phủ định) finish until late. → may not / might not
• It ___ (could, snow) tonight, so drive carefully. → could snow
• ❌ It might rains tomorrow. → It might rain tomorrow. (might + V nguyên mẫu.)
• ❌ She may to visit us next week. → She may visit us next week. (may + V nguyên mẫu, không có to.)
• ❌ I don't might come to the meeting. → I might not come to the meeting. (Phủ định: might not.)
Quá khứ hoàn thành tiếp diễn B2
had been + V-ing: She had been working for hours.
Thường đi với for / since và một mốc trong quá khứ (when, by the time, before).
Việc kéo dài tới một thời điểm trong quá khứ (nhấn mạnh quá trình). · Giải thích kết quả nhìn thấy trong quá khứ: His eyes were red because he had been crying. · Không dùng với động từ trạng thái (know, own, believe): “I had been knowing” là sai → I had known.
• By the time the doctor arrived, the patient ___ for two hours. → had been waiting (✗ has been waiting · ✗ was waiting)
• He was tired because he ___ all night. → had been studying (✗ has studied · ✗ studies)
• I ___ him for years before we became friends. → had known (✗ had been knowing · ✗ have known)
• Her hands were dirty because she ___ in the garden. → had been working (✗ has been working · ✗ is working)
• They ___ (play) tennis for an hour when it started to rain. → had been playing / 'd been playing
• Why were you so tired? ___ (you / run)? → Had you been running
• The road was blocked because workers ___ (repair) it all morning. → had been repairing / 'd been repairing
• ❌ When I arrived, she has been waiting for an hour. → When I arrived, she had been waiting for an hour. (Mốc trong quá khứ → had been waiting.)
• ❌ I had been owning the car for ten years when it broke down. → I had owned the car for ten years when it broke down. (own là động từ trạng thái → had owned.)
• ❌ His eyes were red because he cried. → His eyes were red because he had been crying. (Nguyên nhân kéo dài trước thời điểm quá khứ → had been crying.)

💼 TOEIC Nghe · Part 3 / 4 có bảng và hội thoại 3 người (v124) (9)

Câu "Look at the graphic" (đề thật từ 2016) và hội thoại 3 người. Kiểm: đáp án câu bảng phải cần CẢ bảng và lời nghe; tên người / giới tính khớp câu hỏi (A nữ, B nam, C nữ thứ hai); lời tự nhiên.

t3-09 Part 3
Lời

A: Hi, Sam and Priya. Thanks for staying late. The client wants to see the new logo designs by Monday instead of Wednesday. B: Monday? That's only three days away. We haven't finished the color versions yet. C: I can work on the colors this weekend if someone else prepares the presentation slides. A: That would be great, Priya. Sam, could you handle the slides? I'll call the client and confirm the meeting time.


• What problem do the speakers discuss? → A deadline has been moved earlier. (✗ A client has canceled a project. · ✗ A designer is leaving the company. · ✗ The presentation room is unavailable.)
• What does the woman named Priya offer to do? → Work during the weekend (✗ Call the client · ✗ Prepare the slides · ✗ Change the logo design completely)
• What will the first woman do next? → Contact the client (✗ Finish the color versions · ✗ Book a meeting room · ✗ Review the slides)
t3-21 Part 3
Bảng: Room availability: Thursday · Room | Seats | Free · 101 | 6 | 9:00–11:00 · 204 | 12 | 1:00–3:00 · 305 | 20 | 10:00–12:00 · 410 | 30 | 2:00–5:00
Lời

A: Hi, Daniel. I'm organizing the training session on the new expense software this Thursday. Could you help me book a room? B: Sure. How many people are coming? A: Eighteen so far, and it has to be after lunch, because the sales team is out of the office until noon. B: Then there's only one room that works. I'll reserve it now. Do you need a projector? A: Yes, please. And could you ask the café to send up coffee at three?


• What is the woman organizing? → A training session (✗ A sales meeting · ✗ A product launch · ✗ A team lunch)
• Look at the graphic. Which room will the speakers most likely use? → Room 410 (✗ Room 101 · ✗ Room 204 · ✗ Room 305)
• What does the woman ask the man to do? → Arrange for coffee (✗ Install some software · ✗ Contact the sales team · ✗ Print some handouts)
t3-22 Part 3
Bảng: Trains to Riverside: Friday · Departs | Arrives · 7:15 | 9:40 · 8:05 | 10:20 · 8:50 | 11:05 · 9:30 | 11:55
Lời

A: Ken, are we still meeting the clients in Riverside on Friday? B: Yes, at eleven. Their office is about a ten-minute walk from the station, so we should arrive by half past ten. A: OK. I'd take the earliest train, but I have a video call with our Tokyo office until seven-thirty. B: Then we'll take the next one. I'll buy the tickets online tonight and e-mail yours to you.


• Why are the speakers going to Riverside? → To meet some clients (✗ To attend a conference · ✗ To visit a factory · ✗ To interview a job applicant)
• Look at the graphic. Which train will the speakers take? → The 8:05 train (✗ The 7:15 train · ✗ The 8:50 train · ✗ The 9:30 train)
• What will the man do tonight? → Buy tickets (✗ Call the Tokyo office · ✗ Prepare a presentation · ✗ Book a hotel)
t3-23 Part 3
Bảng: Folding chairs: quantity discounts · Chairs ordered | Discount · 1–49 | 0% · 50–99 | 5% · 100–199 | 10% · 200 or more | 15%
Lời

A: Hello, this is Mira Chen from the Lakeside Hotel. I'd like to order some folding chairs for our ballroom. B: Certainly. How many do you need? A: We're expecting eighty guests at a conference, and we want forty more for a second room. So, a hundred and twenty in total. B: Great. I'll e-mail you an order form. Delivery usually takes five business days. A: That's fine. The conference isn't until the end of next month.


• Where does the woman work? → At a hotel (✗ At a furniture store · ✗ At a university · ✗ At a delivery company)
• Look at the graphic. What discount will the woman most likely receive? → 10% (✗ 0% · ✗ 5% · ✗ 15%)
• What does the man say he will send? → An order form (✗ A product catalog · ✗ A discount code · ✗ A delivery schedule)
t3-24 Part 3
Lời

A: Thanks for joining me, Tom and Lisa. As you know, we're moving to the new building on the fifteenth. B: Do we know who's packing the computers? Last time it took the IT team two days to set everything up again. C: I spoke to the IT manager this morning. They'll come in over the weekend, so nothing will be down on Monday. A: Perfect. Then, Tom, could you make sure every box has a label with the employee's name and new desk number? B: Sure. I'll pick up some labels from the supply room this afternoon.


• What are the speakers mainly discussing? → An office move (✗ A computer upgrade · ✗ A new employee · ✗ A weekend event)
• What does Lisa say about the IT team? → They will work over the weekend. (✗ They need two more days. · ✗ They are short of staff. · ✗ They will buy new computers.)
• What will the man do this afternoon? → Get some labels (✗ Pack his computer · ✗ Visit the new building · ✗ Call the IT manager)
t3-25 Part 3
Lời

A: Excuse me. I booked a room with a sea view, but the room I was given faces the parking lot. B: I'm very sorry about that. Let me ask my manager. Ms. Park, do we have any sea-view rooms left? C: I'm afraid they're all taken tonight. But we can move you tomorrow morning, and tonight's dinner at our restaurant will be on us. A: That sounds fair. What time can I move? B: Any time after ten. I'll have someone carry your bags to the new room.


• What problem does the guest mention? → Her room does not have the view she booked. (✗ Her room is too noisy. · ✗ Her bags were lost. · ✗ Her booking was canceled.)
• What does the manager offer? → A free dinner (✗ A cheaper room · ✗ A late checkout · ✗ Free parking)
• What will happen after ten o'clock? → The guest can change rooms. (✗ The restaurant will open. · ✗ The manager will call the guest. · ✗ A taxi will arrive.)
t4-21 Part 4
Bảng: Green Business Forum: program · Time | Talk | Speaker · 9:30 | Opening remarks | Lena Ortiz · 11:00 | Green packaging | Amir Patel · 1:30 | Saving energy in offices | Mark Hill · 3:00 | Smart transport | Sofia Grant
Lời

Good morning, everyone, and welcome to the Green Business Forum. Before we begin, there's a change to today's program. Dr. Amir Patel's train from Boston has been delayed, so he won't arrive until around noon. He'll give his talk in the one-thirty slot, and the speaker who was scheduled for that time has kindly agreed to present at eleven instead. Lunch will be served in the main hall at twelve-thirty. Now, let's begin with our opening remarks.


• Where is the talk most likely taking place? → At a business conference (✗ At a train station · ✗ On a factory tour · ✗ At a job fair)
• Why has the schedule changed? → A speaker's train is late. (✗ A room is not available. · ✗ A session was canceled. · ✗ The opening started late.)
• Look at the graphic. Who will speak at eleven o'clock? → Mark Hill (✗ Lena Ortiz · ✗ Amir Patel · ✗ Sofia Grant)
t4-22 Part 4
Bảng: Building directory · Floor | Tenant / room · 1 | Reception, café · 2 | Training center · 3 | Brightline Law · 4 | Conference rooms · 5 | Northpoint Media
Lời

Hello, Mr. Romero. This is Julia Brooks from building management. I'm calling about your team meeting next Tuesday morning. The conference rooms will be closed that day because we're replacing the carpets, so we've moved your meeting to the training center. It has the same equipment, including a projector and a large screen. Also, please note that the café will open late that day, at eleven. If you have any questions, call me at extension three-one-two.


• Why is the speaker calling? → To report a change of location (✗ To book a training course · ✗ To ask about a projector · ✗ To complain about noise)
• Look at the graphic. On which floor will the meeting be held? → Floor 2 (✗ Floor 1 · ✗ Floor 3 · ✗ Floor 4)
• What does the speaker say about the café? → It will open later than usual. (✗ It will be closed all day. · ✗ It will serve free coffee. · ✗ It has moved to another floor.)
t4-23 Part 4
Bảng: Running shoe sales (pairs) · Month | Sales · January | 420 · February | 510 · March | 260 · April | 640
Lời

Good afternoon. Let's look at the sales figures for our new line of running shoes. Overall, the results are encouraging. The one weak month was when our largest store, on Main Street, closed for two weeks for renovation. Sales picked up again right after it reopened, and April was our best month so far. Because of this success, we're going to offer the shoes in two new colors this summer. Before our next meeting, I'd like each of you to come up with ideas for an advertising campaign.


• What product is the speaker discussing? → Running shoes (✗ Sports clothing · ✗ Store furniture · ✗ Fitness equipment)
• Look at the graphic. When was the Main Street store closed? → March (✗ January · ✗ February · ✗ April)
• What are the listeners asked to do? → Think of ideas for advertising (✗ Visit the Main Street store · ✗ Choose two new colors · ✗ Prepare a sales report)

💼 TOEIC · 180 từ công sở mới (v124) (15)

15 chủ đề mới (khách sạn, công nghệ, sản xuất, báo cáo, giao tiếp, đào tạo, an toàn, ngân hàng, bất động sản, bán lẻ, mua hàng, quản lý, môi trường, phúc lợi, truyền thông). Kiểm: nghĩa Việt đúng trong ngữ cảnh công sở, câu ví dụ tự nhiên.
