📰 Đọc Nền B1 → C2 · 20 bài luyện mới (v125) (20)

Mỗi cấp 5 bài. Kiểm: độ khó hợp cấp, đáp án đúng và duy nhất, phương án nhiễu sai rõ, số liệu / sự kiện thật đúng (vd. Mercator, phán quyết 2014 của EU, nghiên cứu ví tiền thất lạc), câu suy luận (🧠) suy ra được từ bài.

The Library That Lends Tools B1 · Thư viện cho mượn dụng cụ
Bài

Most people have a drill at home that they use for about ten minutes a year. In the town of Millbrook, a group of neighbours decided this was a waste of money and space. Two years ago they opened a 'tool library' in an empty shop on the high street. It works like a normal library. Members pay a small fee of ten pounds a year and can borrow up to three tools at a time for one week. There are more than four hundred items, from ladders and saws to sewing machines and tents. Most of them were given by local people who no longer needed them. The library is run by volunteers, and every Saturday morning a retired builder called Peter teaches beginners how to use the tools safely. "Some people are nervous the first time," he says. "By the end of the morning, they're putting up shelves." Not everything has gone smoothly. In the first year, several tools came back broken, so now members must pay for repairs if something is damaged.


• Where did most of the tools come from? → Local people gave them (✗ The town council bought them · ✗ A builder's shop sold them cheaply)
• What does Peter do on Saturday mornings? → He shows beginners how to use tools safely (✗ He repairs broken tools · ✗ He collects fees from new members)
• Why must members now pay for repairs? → Some tools were returned damaged (✗ The fee is too low to cover costs · ✗ Peter can no longer fix them for free)
• 🧠 What can we infer about the tool library? → It has helped people learn new skills. (✗ It has made a large profit. · ✗ It is used mostly by builders.)
An Email from a Host Family B1 · Thư của gia đình nhận học sinh ở nhờ
Bài

Hi Minh, We're really looking forward to meeting you next Saturday! Just a few things before you arrive. Our house is about twenty minutes from the airport by car. Unfortunately, neither of us can pick you up, because Sarah works on Saturdays and I'll be at our son's football match. But don't worry – the airport bus stops right at the end of our street. Get off at Park Road, and our house is the blue one opposite the bakery. You'll have your own room on the top floor, with a desk for studying. The internet password is on a card on the desk. We usually have dinner at half past six. Please let us know if there's anything you don't eat – Sarah is a vegetarian, so we eat a lot of vegetables anyway! Your school is a fifteen-minute walk away. On Monday morning I'll walk there with you so you can learn the way. See you soon, David


• How will Minh get to the house? → By airport bus (✗ David will drive him · ✗ Sarah will meet him at the airport)
• Why does David ask about food? → He wants to know if there is anything Minh doesn't eat (✗ He wants Minh to cook one evening · ✗ He thinks Minh is a vegetarian)
• What will happen on Monday morning? → David will show Minh the way to school (✗ Minh will take the bus to school · ✗ Sarah will take Minh shopping)
• 🧠 What kind of person does David seem to be? → Friendly and helpful. (✗ Strict and unfriendly. · ✗ Too busy to care about Minh.)
A Week Without My Phone B1 · Một tuần không có điện thoại
Bài

Last month my phone fell into a river during a camping trip. The shop said a new one would take a week to arrive, so I had no choice: I had to live without it. The first two days were difficult. I kept putting my hand in my pocket to check messages that weren't there. I missed a friend's party because I didn't know it had moved to a different café. And without a map on my phone, I got lost on my way to a job interview and arrived ten minutes late. But by Wednesday, something changed. On the bus I looked out of the window instead of at a screen, and I noticed a small bookshop I had passed a hundred times. I read two novels that week – more than I'd read in the whole of the previous year. I also slept better, because I wasn't checking social media in bed. When the new phone arrived, I was happy to have it back. But I've made one rule: it stays in the kitchen at night.


• Why did the writer arrive late for a job interview? → Because the writer got lost (✗ Because the bus broke down · ✗ Because the interview had moved)
• What did the writer notice on the bus? → A bookshop (✗ A friend from the party · ✗ A shop selling phones)
• 🧠 How does the writer feel about the week now? → It had some good results. (✗ It was a complete waste of time. · ✗ Everyone should stop using phones.)
From Rice Field to Online Shop B1 · Từ ruộng lúa đến cửa hàng trên mạng
Bài

For thirty years, Mr Tuan sold his rice to a trader who came to his village in the Mekong Delta after every harvest. The trader decided the price, and it was always low. In 2021, Mr Tuan's daughter Linh came home from university in Can Tho with an idea. She took photos of the fields, of the family drying rice in the sun and of her father's hands holding the grain. Then she opened a small online shop and posted the photos with the story of the farm. At first, only friends and relatives bought anything. But one video of her father explaining how he grew rice without chemicals was shared thousands of times. Within a month, they had orders from Hanoi, Da Nang and even Singapore. Now the family sells about half of their rice online, at almost twice the old price. It isn't easy: Linh spends every evening answering messages and packing orders. "But for the first time," she says, "my father decides what his work is worth."


• What was the problem with selling rice to the trader? → The price was always low (✗ He only came every few years · ✗ He wanted rice grown without chemicals)
• What helped the shop become popular? → A video that many people shared (✗ Advertisements in Singapore · ✗ A report on national television)
• 🧠 What does Linh mean in her last sentence? → Her father now controls the price of his rice. (✗ Her father works harder than before. · ✗ Her father wants to stop farming.)
Ask the Expert: Saving for a Trip B1 · Hỏi chuyên gia: tiết kiệm tiền đi du lịch
Bài

Question: I'm nineteen and I want to travel around Southeast Asia next summer, but I never have any money left at the end of the month. How can I save? – Bao Answer: Dear Bao, first of all, find out where your money goes. For two weeks, write down everything you spend, even a coffee. Most people are surprised by how much the small things add up to. Next, decide how much the trip will cost and divide that by the number of months until you leave. That's your monthly target. On the day you get paid, move that amount into a separate savings account straight away. Don't wait to see what's left at the end of the month, because there won't be anything! Finally, look for cheaper ways to have fun. Cook with friends instead of eating out, and borrow books instead of buying them. Saving shouldn't mean staying at home alone every weekend. Good luck – and send us a postcard!


• What should Bao do first? → Write down everything he spends for two weeks (✗ Open a new bank account · ✗ Stop buying coffee)
• When should Bao move money into his savings account? → As soon as he is paid (✗ At the end of each month · ✗ When he has decided where to go)
• What does the expert say about having fun? → Bao can still enjoy himself without spending much (✗ Bao should stay at home every weekend · ✗ Bao should only go out when friends pay)
• 🧠 What does the expert suggest about small purchases? → Together they cost more than people think. (✗ They are not worth writing down. · ✗ They are the reason people travel.)
Citizen Scientists B2 · Người dân làm nhà khoa học
Bài

Every winter, tens of thousands of ordinary people across North America spend a day counting birds. They are not professional scientists, but the information they collect has become one of the most valuable records of how bird populations are changing. This is citizen science: research in which members of the public help to gather or analyse data. The idea is not new – amateur astronomers have been reporting comets for centuries – but smartphones have transformed it. Today, volunteers photograph insects, record the calls of frogs, measure rainfall or classify images of distant galaxies from their sofas. Projects that once needed decades of fieldwork can now collect millions of observations in a single season. Critics point out that volunteers make mistakes. A beginner might confuse two similar species, or report a bird that was never there. Researchers have responded by building checks into their projects: rare sightings are reviewed by experts, and many apps ask for a photo as evidence. For the volunteers themselves, the benefits often go beyond science. Many say that taking part has changed the way they see their own neighbourhood. "I'd walked past that pond every day for years," one frog-counter explained, "and I'd never once stopped to listen."


• According to the article, what has changed citizen science the most? → Smartphones (✗ Better-trained volunteers · ✗ Government funding)
• How have researchers dealt with volunteers' mistakes? → Unusual reports are checked by experts (✗ Only experienced volunteers may take part · ✗ Data from beginners is not used)
• 🧠 What does the frog-counter's comment suggest? → The project made the volunteer notice the local area. (✗ The volunteer found counting frogs boring. · ✗ The pond had only recently been built.)
The Real Cost of Fast Fashion B2 · Cái giá thật của thời trang nhanh
Bài

A T-shirt for the price of a sandwich sounds like a bargain. But according to many environmental groups, the true cost of 'fast fashion' – cheap clothes produced quickly to follow the latest trends – is paid elsewhere. Making clothes uses enormous amounts of water and energy. Cotton is a thirsty crop, and many synthetic fabrics are made from oil. Because fast-fashion items are designed to be worn only a few times, a large share of them end up in landfill or are burnt within a year or two of being bought. There are human costs too. To keep prices low, some factories pay wages that are barely enough to live on, and safety rules are not always enforced. Some brands now offer recycling schemes or 'sustainable' collections. Sceptics argue that this is largely marketing: a recycling bin in the corner of a shop does little if the same shop is releasing new designs every week. The most effective change, they say, is the simplest one – buying fewer clothes and wearing them for longer. Consumers seem to be listening, slowly. The market for second-hand clothing has grown rapidly in recent years, helped by apps that make selling an old jacket as easy as posting a photo.


• Why do many fast-fashion clothes end up in landfill? → They are made to be worn only a few times (✗ They cannot be recycled at all · ✗ Shops throw away clothes they cannot sell)
• What do sceptics think about brands' recycling schemes? → They are mostly about image, not real change (✗ They are the best solution available · ✗ They are too expensive for shoppers)
• What is suggested as the most effective change? → Buying less and keeping clothes longer (✗ Choosing only sustainable collections · ✗ Selling clothes through apps)
• 🧠 Why does the writer put 'sustainable' in quotation marks? → To suggest the claim may not be fully true. (✗ To show it is a technical term. · ✗ To show consumers invented the word.)
Should Museums Give Objects Back? B2 · Bảo tàng có nên trả lại hiện vật?
Bài

Many of the world's great museums contain objects that were taken from other countries during periods of war or colonial rule. For decades, requests to return them were usually refused. Recently, however, the debate has shifted, and a number of museums have begun to send items home. Supporters of return argue that these objects are not simply works of art but part of a living culture. A carved figure in a glass case in Europe may be a sacred object to the community it came from. Keeping it, they say, continues the injustice of the original taking. Museums that resist often make practical arguments. They point out that their collections are seen by millions of visitors from all over the world, and that the objects are carefully protected. Some also worry that if they return one item, they will face hundreds of similar claims. A middle way is appearing in some places: long-term loans, shared research projects and digital copies that allow objects to be studied anywhere. Critics on both sides are unconvinced. For some, anything less than full return is not enough; for others, even loans set a dangerous example. What seems clear is that museums can no longer ignore the question of how their collections were acquired.


• Why do supporters want objects returned? → They belong to the culture of the communities they came from (✗ Museums cannot protect them properly · ✗ Too few people visit museums today)
• What is one worry of museums that resist? → One return may lead to many more requests (✗ Visitors will stop coming · ✗ The objects will be damaged on the journey)
• What does the writer conclude? → Museums must now face the question of where their objects came from (✗ Digital copies have solved the problem · ✗ Most objects will soon be returned)
• 🧠 What can we infer about the 'middle way'? → It does not fully satisfy either side. (✗ It has ended the debate. · ✗ It is supported by most critics.)
The City at Night B2 · Những người làm ca đêm của thành phố
Bài

At three in the morning, most of the city is asleep. But in a bakery near the river, Hung has already been working for two hours, shaping dough for the six o'clock opening. Across town, a cleaner is polishing the floors of an empty office tower, a nurse is checking on patients in intensive care, and a lorry driver is unloading fresh fish at the wholesale market. Night workers keep modern cities running, yet they are largely invisible. Public transport is limited after midnight, so many rely on motorbikes or expensive taxis. Shops and clinics are often closed by the time their shifts end. Working at night also has a cost to health. Research has linked long-term night work to sleep problems, weight gain and a higher risk of heart disease, partly because the body's internal clock is disturbed. Experts recommend keeping the bedroom completely dark during the day and trying to sleep at the same time even on days off. Some cities are beginning to respond. A few now have 'night mayors' – officials whose job is to represent people who live and work after dark, from improving late-night buses to making sure there are places to eat. For people like Hung, it is about time. "We feed the city," he says, "but the city forgets we exist."


• What problem do many night workers have getting home? → There is little public transport late at night (✗ Taxis refuse to take them · ✗ Their workplaces are far from the river)
• What advice do experts give night workers? → Sleep at the same time, even on days off (✗ Work nights for only a few months · ✗ Keep a light on while sleeping in the day)
• 🧠 How does Hung feel about his situation? → He feels his work is not recognised. (✗ He enjoys working while the city is quiet. · ✗ He wants to find a day job soon.)
Why Habits Are Hard to Change B2 · Vì sao thói quen khó thay đổi
Bài

Think about how you brushed your teeth this morning. You probably can't remember, and that is exactly the point. Psychologists estimate that a large part of what we do each day is done out of habit, with little conscious thought. A habit forms when an action is repeated many times in the same situation. Over time, the situation itself – waking up, sitting at your desk, hearing your phone buzz – starts to trigger the action automatically. This saves the brain a great deal of effort, which is why habits are so useful. Unfortunately, the same process applies to bad habits as to good ones. This explains why simply deciding to change rarely works. Willpower is limited, and when we are tired or stressed, we fall back on whatever is automatic. Researchers suggest a more practical approach: change the situation instead of fighting the habit. If you want to stop checking your phone at work, leave it in a drawer. If you want to go running, put your shoes by the door the night before. How long does it take to build a new habit? Popular books often claim twenty-one days, but one well-known study found the average was closer to two months – and it varied enormously from person to person.


• Why are habits useful, according to the article? → They save the brain effort (✗ They help us remember things · ✗ They make us more creative)
• What do researchers suggest for changing a habit? → Change the situation that triggers it (✗ Use more willpower · ✗ Change several habits at once)
• What did one study find about building a new habit? → It took about two months on average (✗ It took exactly twenty-one days · ✗ It took the same time for everyone)
• 🧠 Why does the writer begin by asking about brushing your teeth? → To show how little we notice our habits. (✗ To give advice about health. · ✗ To show that habits are easy to change.)
The Placebo Puzzle C1 · Câu đố về thuốc giả
Bài

For most of the twentieth century, the placebo effect was regarded as a nuisance – something to be eliminated from drug trials so that the 'real' effect of a medicine could be measured. Patients who improved after taking a sugar pill were assumed to have imagined their recovery, or to have been on the mend anyway. That view now looks too simple. Brain-imaging studies have shown that when people expect pain relief, their brains release natural painkillers, so the relief they report is not merely imaginary. Expectation, it turns out, can produce measurable changes in the body, particularly for symptoms such as pain, nausea and fatigue. Stranger still are experiments with so-called 'open-label' placebos, in which patients are told quite frankly that the pills they are taking contain no active ingredient. In several small trials, people with chronic back pain or digestive disorders still reported improvements. Researchers suspect that the ritual of treatment – the consultation, the instructions, the act of taking something regularly – may matter as much as the pill itself. None of this means that placebos can shrink tumours or cure infections; there is no good evidence that they can. But it does suggest that the way medicine is delivered – how much time a doctor spends listening, how a treatment is explained – may influence outcomes far more than we once assumed.


• How was the placebo effect viewed in the past? → As something that got in the way of testing drugs (✗ As a useful treatment for pain · ✗ As proof that most medicines did not work)
• What did brain-imaging studies show? → Expecting relief can cause real physical changes (✗ Patients usually imagine their improvement · ✗ Placebos work better than painkillers)
• What is surprising about 'open-label' placebos? → They seem to help even when patients know they are fake (✗ They are more expensive than real drugs · ✗ They only work for serious diseases)
• What does the writer suggest in the final paragraph? → How care is given may affect how patients recover (✗ Placebos could replace many medicines · ✗ Doctors should prescribe sugar pills more often)
The Economics of Queuing C1 · Kinh tế học của việc xếp hàng
Bài

Nobody enjoys waiting in line, yet queues are one of the oldest ways of sharing out something scarce. Unlike prices, which favour those with money, a queue favours those with time – a principle many people regard as fairer, even if it is hardly efficient. Economists have long pointed out the hidden cost. An hour spent waiting for concert tickets is an hour not spent working, studying or resting, and the value of that lost time is rarely counted. In theory, auctioning the tickets would be more efficient: they would go to those willing to pay most. In practice, the idea provokes outrage, because it appears to turn a fan's enthusiasm into a privilege of wealth. Businesses have found ways to profit from this tension. Theme parks sell 'fast passes' that allow visitors to skip the line; airlines offer priority boarding. Each innovation is defended as offering customers choice, but critics argue that it quietly converts a shared, if irritating, experience into a two-tier system. Psychologists add another twist: how long a wait feels matters more than how long it actually is. Unoccupied time seems longer than occupied time, uncertain waits seem longer than known ones, and unfair waits – when someone pushes in – are the most frustrating of all. This is why some airports have deliberately made passengers walk further to collect their luggage: the walk feels like progress, whereas standing at an empty carousel feels like waiting.


• Why do many people think queues are fairer than prices? → They do not depend on how much money people have (✗ They are the most efficient system · ✗ They reward people who arrive in groups)
• Why does auctioning tickets cause anger? → It seems to give wealthy people an advantage (✗ It takes longer than queuing · ✗ It is harder to organise)
• What do critics say about 'fast passes'? → They create two classes of customer (✗ They make queues move faster for everyone · ✗ They are too expensive to be popular)
• Why might an airport make passengers walk further? → Walking makes the wait feel shorter (✗ It reduces crowding at the carousel · ✗ It helps passengers find the right exit)
When Languages Fall Silent C1 · Khi một ngôn ngữ im tiếng
Bài

Of the roughly seven thousand languages spoken today, linguists estimate that around half may disappear by the end of this century. Most are spoken by small communities, and many are no longer being learnt by children – the clearest sign that a language is in danger. Why should the rest of us care? One answer is scientific. Each language encodes a different way of organising experience: some have no words for 'left' and 'right' and describe space entirely in terms of compass directions; others mark in their grammar whether the speaker witnessed an event or merely heard about it. When a language vanishes before it has been recorded, that evidence of human possibility is lost for good. For speakers, however, the loss is rarely abstract. Language carries songs, place names, jokes and knowledge of local plants that may exist nowhere else. People who have lost their ancestral language often describe it as losing a connection to their grandparents. Revival is difficult but not impossible. Hebrew, which for centuries was used mainly in religious texts, became the everyday language of millions. Welsh has gained speakers through schools that teach entirely in the language, and Māori has been revived partly through 'language nests', where young children are cared for by fluent elders. What these cases share is not money or technology alone, but a community that decides the language is worth passing on.


• According to the text, what most clearly shows that a language is in danger? → Children are no longer learning it (✗ It has no written form · ✗ It is spoken in only one country)
• Why does the writer mention compass directions? → To show that languages organise experience differently (✗ To explain how languages spread · ✗ To show that some languages are more precise)
• How do people often describe losing their ancestral language? → As losing a link to earlier generations (✗ As a purely scientific loss · ✗ As something that happens without pain)
• What do successful revivals have in common, according to the writer? → A community determined to keep the language alive (✗ Large amounts of government money · ✗ Modern technology such as apps)
The Rise of Slow Travel C1 · Du lịch chậm lên ngôi
Bài

For a generation raised on cheap flights, the ideal holiday often meant seeing as much as possible in as little time as possible: five cities in ten days, each one photographed and ticked off. A growing number of travellers are now rejecting that model in favour of what has been called 'slow travel'. The principle is straightforward. Rather than racing between famous sights, slow travellers stay longer in fewer places, go by train or bus where they can, and try to live, however briefly, like residents – shopping at local markets, learning a few phrases, returning to the same café until the owner recognises them. Part of the appeal is environmental. Flying is by far the most carbon-intensive way to travel, and a single long-haul return flight can produce more emissions than many people in poorer countries generate in a year. But advocates insist that the main reward is the experience itself: a city absorbed over a fortnight, they argue, is remembered quite differently from one glimpsed in an afternoon. Not everyone is persuaded. Slow travel, critics note, demands something many workers simply do not have: long stretches of free time. A family with two weeks' annual leave may feel that being lectured about the virtues of a month-long train journey is a luxury of the privileged. Even its supporters admit that the movement risks becoming another lifestyle label – something to be photographed and posted, rather than practised.


• What kind of holiday does the first paragraph describe? → One that tries to cover many places quickly (✗ One spent in a single city · ✗ One that avoids famous sights)
• According to its advocates, what is the greatest benefit of slow travel? → A deeper experience of places (✗ Lower travel costs · ✗ Less need for planning)
• What is the critics' chief objection? → Many people cannot take enough time off (✗ Trains are more expensive than flights · ✗ Slow travel harms local communities)
• What risk do even supporters accept? → It may become more about image than practice (✗ It may become too popular for small towns · ✗ It may encourage people to fly more)
Trusting Strangers C1 · Niềm tin vào người lạ
Bài

Imagine you find a wallet in the street containing a business card and some cash. Would you return it? And would you be more or less likely to do so if it contained a lot of money? Most people, asked to predict the behaviour of others, assume that more cash means more temptation – and therefore fewer returns. Yet when researchers handed in thousands of apparently lost wallets at banks, hotels and offices in dozens of countries, they found the opposite. In most places, wallets containing money were reported to their owners more often than empty ones, and in follow-up tests, wallets with larger sums were returned more often still. The researchers' explanation centres on self-image. Keeping an empty wallet costs a person little in terms of how they see themselves; keeping one full of someone else's money feels uncomfortably close to theft. Most people, it seems, want to be able to think of themselves as honest, and that desire can outweigh the attraction of easy money. The findings carry a striking implication. If we routinely underestimate the honesty of strangers, we may be designing our institutions – our contracts, our checks, our surveillance – for a population less trustworthy than the one we actually live among. Excessive suspicion has costs of its own: it can insult the honest majority, and there is some evidence that being treated as untrustworthy makes people behave less honestly.


• What did most people expect to happen to wallets containing more money? → Fewer of them would be returned (✗ More of them would be returned · ✗ The amount would make no difference)
• Why, according to the researchers, did people return wallets with money? → They wanted to see themselves as honest (✗ They expected a reward · ✗ They were afraid of being caught)
• What does the writer suggest about our institutions? → They may assume people are less honest than they are (✗ They need more surveillance · ✗ They work well because people are honest)
• Which possible effect of excessive suspicion is mentioned? → It may make people behave less honestly (✗ It may make institutions cheaper to run · ✗ It may reduce the number of lost wallets)
The Myth of the Lone Genius C2 · Huyền thoại thiên tài đơn độc
Bài

Popular histories of science and art are fond of a particular image: the solitary genius, struck by inspiration in a garden or a bath, who single-handedly transforms our understanding of the world. The image is seductive, not least because it flatters our sense that greatness is a property of individuals. It is also, for the most part, misleading. Closer examination of almost any celebrated breakthrough reveals a crowded scene. Newton's remark about standing on the shoulders of giants may have been partly a barbed joke at a rival's expense, but it was apt nonetheless: his work drew on decades of calculation by others. Darwin corresponded with hundreds of naturalists, breeders and collectors, and was prompted to publish only when a younger naturalist, Alfred Russel Wallace, arrived independently at much the same theory. Simultaneous discovery of this kind is so common that some historians regard it as the norm rather than the exception – a sign that ideas emerge when the conditions for them are ripe. Why, then, does the myth persist? Partly because stories require protagonists, and a single name is easier to remember than a network. Partly, too, because institutions reward individuals: prizes are awarded to people, not to the conversations that shaped them. The cost of the myth is not merely historical inaccuracy. Students who believe that real achievement springs from solitary brilliance may conclude, on finding that they need help, that they lack the gift altogether. A more truthful account – of genius as something that happens between people as much as within them – might prove both more accurate and more encouraging.


• Why, according to the writer, is the image of the lone genius attractive? → It suggests that greatness belongs to individuals (✗ It is supported by most historical evidence · ✗ It encourages students to work harder)
• Why does the writer mention Wallace? → As an example of two people reaching the same idea independently (✗ To show that Darwin copied a younger scientist · ✗ To prove that Darwin worked entirely alone)
• According to the writer, why does the myth survive? → Both stories and institutions focus on individuals (✗ Historians have not studied major discoveries closely · ✗ Most scientists prefer to work alone)
• What harm might the myth do to students? → Needing help may make them think they lack talent (✗ They may refuse to work in groups · ✗ They may copy the work of others)
On Apology C2 · Bàn về lời xin lỗi
Bài

Few public rituals are as closely scrutinised, or as frequently botched, as the apology. A company whose product has harmed customers, a politician caught in an indiscretion, a celebrity whose old remarks have resurfaced: each is expected to say sorry, and each is judged less on whether they do so than on how. The most common failure is the conditional apology – 'I'm sorry if anyone was offended' – which manages to express regret while locating the problem in the sensitivity of the listener. Close behind is the passive construction, in which 'mistakes were made', as though by no one in particular. Both are attempts to secure the social benefits of contrition without paying its price, which is the acknowledgement of responsibility. Research on apologies suggests that what people want is surprisingly consistent: a clear statement of what was done wrong, an acceptance of responsibility, an explanation that does not shade into excuse, and some offer of repair. Of these, the acceptance of responsibility appears to matter most; an apology that is eloquent but evasive can be received worse than none at all. Yet there is a tension here. Lawyers frequently advise clients against apologising, fearing that an admission of fault will be used against them in court. Some jurisdictions have responded with so-called 'apology laws', which prevent expressions of sympathy from being treated as evidence of liability. Whether such laws produce more sincere apologies, or merely more carefully worded ones, remains an open question.


• What is wrong with a conditional apology, according to the writer? → It suggests the listener is to blame for being upset (✗ It is too long and formal · ✗ It promises more than it can deliver)
• What do the conditional and the passive apology have in common? → Both avoid accepting responsibility (✗ Both are recommended by lawyers · ✗ Both offer to repair the damage)
• Which element of an apology seems to matter most? → Accepting responsibility (✗ Explaining the reasons · ✗ Offering compensation)
• What is the writer's attitude to 'apology laws'? → Unsure whether they make apologies more sincere (✗ Strongly in favour of them · ✗ Convinced that they encourage dishonesty)
What Maps Leave Out C2 · Những gì bản đồ bỏ qua
Bài

We tend to treat maps as neutral pictures of the world, as if the only question to ask of them were whether they are accurate. Yet every map is an argument. To make one, a cartographer must decide what to include and what to omit, which names to use, where to put the centre and how to flatten a curved surface onto a flat page – and each decision shapes what the reader sees. The most famous example is the Mercator projection, designed in the sixteenth century for navigation. Because it preserves the angles sailors needed for plotting a course, it distorts size, enlarging regions far from the equator: Greenland appears roughly as large as Africa, though Africa is about fourteen times bigger. For centuries this was the image of the world that hung in classrooms, and critics have long argued that it subtly inflated the importance of Europe and North America. Omission can be as telling as distortion. Colonial maps routinely labelled inhabited regions as empty, a convention that made their seizure easier to justify. Even today, digital maps tend to show in rich detail the streets where businesses cluster, while an informal settlement housing thousands of people may barely register. None of this means that maps are lies. It means that they are tools, made by particular people for particular purposes. The sensible response is not to distrust every map, but to ask of each one the questions we would ask of any other text: who made this, for whom, and what did they choose to leave out?


• What does the writer mean by 'every map is an argument'? → Maps reflect choices made by their makers (✗ Maps are usually inaccurate · ✗ Maps cause disagreements between countries)
• Why was the Mercator projection originally designed? → To help sailors navigate (✗ To show the true size of continents · ✗ To be used in classrooms)
• What point does the writer make about colonial maps? → Showing land as empty helped justify taking it (✗ They were more accurate than modern maps · ✗ They left out the names of European cities)
• What does the writer recommend? → Reading maps critically, as we would other texts (✗ Avoiding digital maps · ✗ Using only maps made by local people)
The Right to Be Forgotten C2 · Quyền được lãng quên trên mạng
Bài

For most of human history, forgetting was the default and remembering required effort. Records decayed, gossip faded, and a youthful mistake could, in time, be outlived. The internet has reversed this arrangement. A careless remark, an unflattering photograph or a newspaper report of a minor offence may now remain searchable for decades, attached to a person's name long after the circumstances have changed. It was against this background that, in 2014, the European Union's highest court ruled that individuals could, in certain circumstances, ask search engines to remove links to information about them that was 'inadequate, irrelevant or no longer relevant'. The information itself was not deleted; it simply became harder to find by searching a name. Supporters regard the ruling as a humane recognition that people change, and that a society which never lets anyone escape their past is a harsher one. Critics – among them many journalists and free-speech campaigners – fear that it hands private companies the delicate task of balancing privacy against the public's right to know, and that the powerful will use it to tidy away embarrassing but legitimate stories. Both concerns have some foundation. The difficulty lies in the fact that the same piece of information can be trivial in one context and vital in another: a decades-old bankruptcy may be irrelevant to a neighbour but highly relevant to someone deciding whether to invest in the person's new company. Any workable principle, therefore, must rest less on the information than on the use to which it is put – a distinction that is far easier to state than to apply.


• How has the internet changed the balance between remembering and forgetting? → Remembering has become the default (✗ Forgetting now requires less effort · ✗ Records now decay more quickly)
• What did the 2014 ruling allow? → Asking search engines to remove certain links (✗ Deleting old newspaper articles · ✗ Suing people who share photographs)
• What do critics of the ruling fear? → Powerful people may hide legitimate stories (✗ Search engines will become less popular · ✗ Privacy will be protected too little)
• According to the writer, what should a workable principle focus on? → How the information will be used (✗ How old the information is · ✗ Who originally published it)
The Consolations of Routine C2 · Niềm an ủi của nếp sống đều đặn
Bài

Routine has a poor reputation. In the language of self-help it is a rut to be escaped; in fiction it is the grey background against which adventures begin. To describe someone's life as routine is rarely a compliment. Yet many of those whose work we most admire depended on routines of almost monastic strictness. The novelist who wrote for exactly four hours each morning, the composer who walked the same route every afternoon, the scientist who answered letters only on Sundays – such habits recur so often in the biographies of the productive that they can hardly be coincidental. The explanation may lie in what routine spares us. Every decision, however trivial, demands a little attention. By settling in advance when to work, what to eat and when to sleep, a routine frees that attention for what matters. Far from stifling creativity, it may be its precondition: the mind can wander freely only when the body is on familiar ground. There is also a quieter consolation. In periods of grief or uncertainty, when larger structures of meaning collapse, routine can offer something to hold on to – the kettle boiled, the dog walked, the bed made. Such acts are trivial, but their regularity asserts that life continues. None of this is to deny that routines can harden into prisons, or that some disruption is necessary for growth. The art, perhaps, is to treat routine not as a destination but as scaffolding: a structure that supports the building while it is being made, and that can be adjusted, or removed, when it is no longer needed.


• How is routine usually presented, according to the first paragraph? → As something dull to be escaped (✗ As the secret of a happy life · ✗ As a necessary part of adventure)
• Why does the writer mention a novelist, a composer and a scientist? → To show that productive people often kept strict routines (✗ To argue that routine stops creativity · ✗ To show that artists rarely keep routines)
• How, according to the writer, can routine help creativity? → It saves attention for more important things (✗ It forces the mind to think in new ways · ✗ It removes the need for inspiration)
• What does the image of 'scaffolding' suggest? → Routine should support us but change when it is no longer useful (✗ Routine should be permanent once it is established · ✗ Routine is only useful for people who make things)

A Radio Advert for a Cooking Class B1 · Quảng cáo lớp học nấu ăn
Bài

Have you always wanted to cook delicious Vietnamese food at home, but never known where to start? Then join Mai's Kitchen! Our evening classes are for complete beginners, and each one lasts two hours. In week one, you'll learn to make fresh spring rolls. In week two, you'll make a simple chicken soup. And in the last week, everyone cooks a full meal for their family or friends, who are invited to eat it with us. Classes are small, with no more than eight people, so you'll get plenty of help. All the ingredients are included in the price, but please bring a container to take home anything you don't finish. Book online before the end of this month and get a free recipe book!


• Who are the classes for? → People who have never cooked before (✗ Professional chefs · ✗ Children and their parents)
• What happens in the last week? → Students cook a meal for guests (✗ Students take a cooking test · ✗ Students visit a restaurant)
• What should students bring? → A container (✗ Their own ingredients · ✗ A recipe book)
• 🧠 What can we infer about the price of the class? → Students don't need to buy any food. (✗ It is cheaper for small groups. · ✗ It includes a free container.)

Interview with a Street Artist B2 · Phỏng vấn nghệ sĩ vẽ tranh tường
Bài

A: Your murals are all over the city now. How did you start? B: Honestly, by accident. I was a graphic designer, and a café owner asked me to paint something on the wall outside her shop, because people kept writing graffiti on it. Once the mural was up, the graffiti stopped. A: Why do you think that was? B: I think people respect something that's clearly been made with care. A blank wall almost invites damage. A: Do you ever worry that your work is temporary? B: All the time. Two of my favourite pieces have been painted over by new owners. But that's the nature of street art. It belongs to the street, not to me. I take good photos and move on. A: Any advice for young artists? B: Ask permission. It's slower, but you'll be invited back.


• How did the artist begin painting murals? → A café owner asked him to paint a wall (✗ He won a city competition · ✗ He was caught writing graffiti)
• What happened after the first mural was painted? → People stopped writing graffiti on the wall (✗ The café lost customers · ✗ The owner painted over it)
• How does he feel when his work is painted over? → He accepts it as part of street art (✗ He takes legal action · ✗ He paints the same picture again)
• 🧠 What does the artist's advice suggest about painting without permission? → It may stop artists from getting more work. (✗ It is the fastest way to become famous. · ✗ It is the only way to make real street art.)
A Talk on Rooftop Gardens B2 · Bài nói về vườn trên sân thượng
Bài

Good evening, everyone. When people imagine farming, they usually picture fields in the countryside. But tonight I want to talk about a farm that's eighteen floors above the street. Two years ago, our apartment block turned its unused roof into a vegetable garden. We started with twenty plastic boxes of soil; now we grow tomatoes, herbs, beans and even a few melons. The benefits have surprised us. In summer, the plants keep the top floor noticeably cooler, so those residents use their air conditioners less. Rainwater is collected in tanks and used for watering. But the biggest change has been social. Neighbours who had lived in the same building for years without speaking now meet every Saturday morning to water and harvest. There have been challenges, of course. The wind is strong up there, and we lost our first crop of beans in a storm. If your building is interested, my advice is simple: start small, and check that the roof can take the weight before you buy any soil.


• What is one environmental benefit the speaker mentions? → The top floor uses less air conditioning (✗ The building produces its own electricity · ✗ Fewer cars park near the building)
• According to the speaker, what has been the biggest change? → Neighbours have got to know each other (✗ Residents now eat only home-grown food · ✗ The building has become more valuable)
• What does the speaker advise other buildings to do first? → Check that the roof is strong enough (✗ Plant beans before other vegetables · ✗ Collect rainwater for a year)
• 🧠 How does the speaker seem to feel about the project? → Positive, though aware of the difficulties. (✗ Disappointed by the results. · ✗ Worried that it costs too much.)
Two Students Discuss Exam Revision B2 · Hai sinh viên bàn cách ôn thi
Bài

A: Reading notes over and over doesn't really work, you know. I read an article saying it just makes you feel familiar with the material. B: So what do you do instead? A: I test myself. I close the book and write down everything I can remember, then check what I've missed. It's harder, but it sticks. B: That sounds painful. A: It is, a bit. And I spread it out: an hour a day for two weeks, rather than everything the night before. B: Well, the exam's on Monday, so that's too late for me. But I'll try testing myself this weekend, and maybe get some sleep.

• Why does the woman think re-reading doesn't work well? → It only makes the material seem familiar (✗ It takes too much time · ✗ It makes students too tired)

A Radio Report on a Water Shortage B2 · Phóng sự về nạn thiếu nước
Bài

In the city of Riverton, residents woke up this morning to find their taps running dry for the third time this month. After an unusually dry winter, the reservoir that supplies the city is at just twenty percent of its normal level. The water company has introduced a rota: each district will now have water for twelve hours a day, and a timetable has been published online. Hospitals and schools are not affected. Officials are asking people to take shorter showers and to stop washing cars and watering gardens. However, critics say the crisis was predictable. According to a report published last year, almost a third of the city's water is lost through leaking pipes before it ever reaches a tap. The water company says repairs have started but will take at least five years. For now, many families are buying bottled water and storing rainwater in buckets on their balconies.


• What has caused the water shortage? → A winter with little rain (✗ A broken reservoir wall · ✗ A sudden rise in population)
• Which places still have water all day? → Hospitals and schools (✗ Districts near the reservoir · ✗ Homes with gardens)
• What do critics say about the crisis? → Leaking pipes have made it worse (✗ The company has stopped repairs · ✗ Residents have used too much water)
• 🧠 What can we infer about the next few years in Riverton? → Water problems may continue. (✗ The reservoir will soon be full. · ✗ Bottled water will be banned.)

A Doctor on Listening to Patients C1 · Bác sĩ bàn về việc lắng nghe bệnh nhân
Bài

A: You've argued that doctors interrupt patients too quickly. How quickly are we talking? B: Studies that have recorded consultations found that many doctors interrupt within the first twenty seconds or so. And once interrupted, patients rarely return to what they originally wanted to say. A: But surely doctors are under enormous time pressure. B: They are, and that's precisely the irony. When patients are allowed to finish, most of them speak for well under two minutes. Yet the information they offer in that time often saves far more time later: fewer repeat visits, fewer unnecessary tests. A: So what would you change? B: Training, first of all. Medical students learn an enormous amount about diseases and comparatively little about conversation. And I'd encourage a simple habit. At the end of the patient's account, ask, 'Is there anything else?' And then actually wait for the answer.


• According to the doctor, what happens after patients are interrupted? → They seldom go back to their original point (✗ They usually become angry · ✗ They speak for longer)
• What is ironic about doctors' time pressure? → Letting patients finish can save time overall (✗ Patients prefer short consultations · ✗ Interruptions make consultations longer)
• What does the doctor think medical training lacks? → Enough attention to conversation skills (✗ Enough knowledge of diseases · ✗ Enough practical experience in hospitals)
A Lecture on the History of Advertising C1 · Bài giảng về lịch sử quảng cáo
Bài

Today I'd like to trace how advertising shifted from informing people to persuading them. Early newspaper advertisements, in the eighteenth and much of the nineteenth century, were essentially notices: they told you what was for sale, where, and at what price. The change came with mass production. Once factories could make far more soap or biscuits than local demand required, manufacturers needed to create demand rather than simply meet it. Brands emerged as a way of distinguishing near-identical products, and advertisers began to sell not the product itself but what it supposedly said about the buyer: that they were modern, respectable or attractive. By the early twentieth century, some agencies were openly drawing on psychology. Now, you might assume that today's personalised online advertising is simply the latest stage of the same story. In one sense it is. But there's an important difference. Earlier advertisers addressed the public as a crowd; digital advertisers can address each of us individually, based on what we have searched for and bought. And that raises questions the earlier industry never had to face.


• What were early newspaper advertisements mostly like? → Simple notices about goods and prices (✗ Emotional stories about families · ✗ Pictures with very little text)
• Why, according to the lecturer, did brands emerge? → To make similar products seem different (✗ To lower the price of goods · ✗ To help factories produce more)
• What difference does the lecturer see in digital advertising? → It can target people individually (✗ It no longer uses psychology · ✗ It only gives information about prices)
Colleagues Discuss Moving the Office C1 · Đồng nghiệp bàn việc dời văn phòng
Bài

A: Have you seen the proposal to move the office out to the business park? B: I have. The rent would be almost half what we pay in the centre, so I can see why the directors like it. A: Of course, but have they thought about how people would get there? There's one bus an hour, and it doesn't run after seven. B: They mention a company shuttle from the station. A: Which works if you live near the station. Half the design team live on the other side of the river. I'd add forty minutes to my journey each way. B: That's a fair point. Mind you, the building itself is much better. More light, proper meeting rooms, space to grow. A: I'm not against it in principle. I just think they should survey the staff before signing anything. If we lose two or three experienced designers, the savings on rent won't be worth much. B: I'll suggest that at Thursday's meeting.


• Why do the directors like the proposal? → The rent is much lower (✗ The building is closer to the station · ✗ Staff asked for a move)
• What worries the woman most about the move? → How staff would travel there (✗ The size of the meeting rooms · ✗ The cost of a company shuttle)
• What does the woman want to happen before a decision is made? → Staff should be asked for their views (✗ The design team should move first · ✗ The rent should be negotiated again)
A Talk on Procrastination C1 · Bài nói về thói trì hoãn
Bài

Most of us think of procrastination as a problem of time management: if we only planned better, we'd stop putting things off. But a growing body of research suggests that it's really a problem of managing emotions. We tend to delay tasks that make us feel something unpleasant, such as boredom, anxiety or self-doubt, and the delay brings immediate relief. Unfortunately, that relief is temporary, and the task returns, often accompanied by guilt, which makes it even less appealing. That's the trap. So what helps? Not, it seems, harsher deadlines or more detailed schedules on their own. One approach is to make the first step so small that it barely triggers any discomfort: not 'write the report', but 'open the document and type the title'. Another, rather surprisingly, is self-forgiveness. In one study, students who forgave themselves for procrastinating before their first exam procrastinated less when preparing for the next one. Being kind to yourself, in other words, isn't the same as letting yourself off the hook.


• According to the speaker, what is procrastination really about? → Avoiding unpleasant feelings (✗ Poor planning of time · ✗ Having too much work)
• Why is the relief from delaying a task a trap? → The task comes back with added guilt (✗ It makes deadlines shorter · ✗ It lowers the quality of work)
• What did the study about students find? → Forgiving themselves helped them delay less later (✗ Strict deadlines reduced delays · ✗ Students who planned more did better)
Interview with a Wildlife Photographer C1 · Phỏng vấn nhiếp ảnh gia động vật hoang dã
Bài

A: Your photograph of the snow leopard has been seen millions of times. How long did it take? B: About three weeks of waiting in the cold, for roughly two seconds of the animal being in view. That's typical, really. Wildlife photography is mostly patience. A: There's been some criticism recently of photographers using bait to attract animals. Where do you stand? B: I'm against it. Feeding wild animals changes their behaviour. They lose their fear of people, and that rarely ends well for the animal. If a picture requires that, I'd rather not take it. A: Some people argue that dramatic images help conservation by making people care. B: They do, and I've seen donations rise after a good photo is published. But an image of an animal that has been manipulated tells a kind of lie. If people later discover how it was made, the damage to trust can outweigh the benefit.


• What does the photographer say about the snow leopard picture? → It took weeks of waiting for a very short moment (✗ It was taken on his first day · ✗ It was taken with the help of bait)
• Why is he against using bait? → It changes animals' behaviour in harmful ways (✗ It is too expensive · ✗ It produces poor photographs)
• What risk does he see in manipulated images? → They may damage public trust (✗ They may reduce donations immediately · ✗ They may be too dramatic for viewers)
A Lecture on the Psychology of Risk C2 · Bài giảng về tâm lý học của rủi ro
Bài

Ask people which is more dangerous, flying or driving, and most will correctly say driving. Yet many of those same people feel far more anxious on an aircraft than behind the wheel. This gap between what we know and what we feel is central to the psychology of risk. Several factors seem to inflate our sense of danger. Risks we cannot control feel worse than those we can, which is partly why passengers fear flying while drivers, with their hands on the wheel, tend to feel safe. Risks that are dramatic and memorable, such as a plane crash or a shark attack, loom larger than those that accumulate quietly, such as a poor diet. And unfamiliar risks unsettle us more than familiar ones, even when the familiar ones kill far more people. None of this makes us simply irrational. These intuitions evolved in a world in which vivid, unfamiliar and uncontrollable dangers were often genuinely the most threatening. The difficulty is that modern statistics and ancient instincts frequently point in different directions, and public policy, which responds to fear as much as to evidence, is pulled between the two.


• Why does the lecturer compare flying and driving? → To show that feelings about risk differ from knowledge (✗ To prove that flying is more dangerous · ✗ To explain why people prefer driving)
• According to the lecturer, why do drivers tend to feel safe? → They feel in control (✗ They know the statistics · ✗ Car accidents are rarely reported)
• What is the lecturer's view of our intuitions about risk? → They once made sense but often mislead us today (✗ They are completely irrational · ✗ They are more reliable than statistics)
Two Editors Discuss a Memoir C2 · Hai biên tập viên bàn về một cuốn hồi ký
Bài

A: I've finished the memoir. It's beautifully written, but I'm uneasy about the chapter on her father. B: Because her brother says it didn't happen that way? A: Partly. He's written to us, quite politely, saying that several of the scenes are invented. Conversations he says never took place. B: Memoirists reconstruct dialogue all the time. Nobody remembers a conversation from thirty years ago word for word. Readers understand that. A: Reconstructing is one thing. But if an entire episode is imagined, then surely we're publishing fiction under a false label. B: Or we're publishing one person's truth, which is what a memoir has always been. Her brother's memory isn't automatically more reliable than hers. A: Agreed. Still, I'd feel more comfortable with an author's note acknowledging that others remember events differently. B: I could live with that, as long as it doesn't read as an apology. The book deserves better than that.


• What has the author's brother claimed? → Some scenes in the book were made up (✗ The book contains private letters · ✗ Their father wants the book withdrawn)
• What is the man's view of reconstructed dialogue? → It is normal and readers accept it (✗ It should always be avoided · ✗ It is acceptable only in fiction)
• What do the editors finally agree on? → Adding a note about differing memories (✗ Removing the chapter about the father · ✗ Asking the brother to write a reply)
A Talk on Silence in Music C2 · Bài nói về khoảng lặng trong âm nhạc
Bài

We usually think of music as sound, and silence as its absence: the empty space before the first note and after the last. But composers and performers have long known that silence is part of the material. A pause before the final chord can create more tension than any crescendo; a rest in the middle of a phrase can make the following note feel inevitable or surprising, depending on its length. The most famous experiment along these lines is John Cage's 'silent' piece, first performed in 1952, in which a pianist sits at the instrument for four minutes and thirty-three seconds without playing. Audiences were often outraged, assuming it was a joke at their expense. Cage's point, however, was more subtle: there is no such thing as true silence. The 'silence' of the piece is filled with coughs, rain on the roof and the creak of seats, sounds that we normally filter out. Whether one regards the piece as profound or as a provocation, it does something few concerts manage: it makes the audience listen to everything.


• What point does the speaker make about pauses in music? → They can build tension and shape what follows (✗ They are mainly for performers to rest · ✗ They matter less than loud passages)
• How did audiences often react to Cage's piece? → They thought they were being mocked (✗ They listened in complete silence · ✗ They asked for it to be repeated)
• What was Cage's real point, according to the speaker? → True silence does not exist (✗ Pianists should play less · ✗ Concerts should be shorter)
Interview with an Urban Planner C2 · Phỏng vấn nhà quy hoạch đô thị
Bài

A: You've said that building more roads doesn't reduce traffic. That sounds counter-intuitive. B: It does, but the evidence is remarkably consistent. When you add road capacity, journeys initially get faster, so more people choose to drive, at more times of day, and within a few years congestion is back where it started. Planners call it induced demand. A: So what's the alternative? Simply letting traffic grind to a halt? B: Not at all. The alternative is to give people realistic choices. If a bus is frequent and reliable, or a cycle route is safe, a proportion of drivers will switch, and that frees up the road for those who genuinely need it. Delivery vans, for instance. A: Critics would say that's social engineering. B: Every transport decision is social engineering. A six-lane motorway shapes behaviour just as much as a cycle lane does. The question isn't whether we influence how people travel, because we always do. It's which outcomes we're choosing.


• According to the planner, what happens when road capacity is increased? → Traffic eventually returns to its earlier level (✗ Journeys become permanently faster · ✗ Fewer people choose to drive)
• What alternative does the planner propose? → Offering good alternatives to driving (✗ Banning cars from city centres · ✗ Charging drivers higher taxes)
• How does he respond to the charge of 'social engineering'? → All transport decisions influence behaviour (✗ He admits that it is a danger · ✗ He says planners should avoid it)
A Radio Essay on Handwriting C2 · Tiểu luận trên đài về chữ viết tay
Bài

When did you last write a letter by hand? For many of us, handwriting has shrunk to shopping lists and signatures, and some schools have reduced the time spent teaching it. Is anything lost? The evidence is more interesting than either nostalgists or enthusiasts for technology tend to admit. Several studies suggest that students who take notes by hand remember lectures somewhat better than those who type, perhaps because writing is slower and forces them to summarise rather than transcribe. Other researchers have failed to replicate the effect, or have found it to be small. What seems clearer is that learning to form letters by hand helps young children recognise them: the movement of the hand appears to reinforce the shape in memory. Beyond the research, there is something harder to measure. A handwritten note carries traces of its writer: hurry, care, hesitation, a crossing-out. Perhaps that is why, in an age of instant messages, a handwritten card can still feel like a small act of generosity. It costs the sender time, and time, these days, is what we value most.


• Why might handwritten notes help students remember? → Writing slowly forces them to summarise (✗ Handwriting is easier to read later · ✗ Students concentrate better without screens)
• What does the speaker say about the research on note-taking? → Its findings have not always been confirmed (✗ It proves that typing is harmful · ✗ It has only studied young children)
• Why, according to the speaker, can a handwritten card feel generous? → It shows the sender has given up time (✗ It is more expensive than a message · ✗ It is easier to keep than a message)

🌏 IELTS Nghe · 3 phần mới (v126) (3)
