Kiểm: đáp án đúng và duy nhất, đáp án điền có nguyên văn trong lời nghe, số liệu thật đúng (rái cá ăn ~1/4 cân nặng mỗi ngày, sóng nhiệt biển Bắc California).

Part 2: Volunteering at a music festival
Lời

Good evening, everyone, and thank you for coming to this information session. I'm Karen Doyle, and I coordinate the volunteers at the Riverside Music Festival. This will be our twelfth festival, and last year we had just over three hundred volunteers. Without them, the festival simply couldn't happen. First, some practical points. The festival runs for three days, from Friday the eighteenth to Sunday the twentieth of July. We ask every volunteer to work at least two shifts of six hours. In return, you get a free ticket for the whole weekend, meals during your shifts, and a festival T-shirt, which you must wear while you're working, so that visitors can recognise you. A lot of people ask about camping. Volunteers can stay at the staff campsite free of charge, but you need to bring your own tent. The campsite is quieter than the main one, although I can't promise you'll get much sleep! Training is compulsory. There's a two-hour session on Saturday the twelfth of July at the community hall. If you can't make that date, there's an online version, but you must complete it at least three days before the festival starts. Now, let me tell you about the different teams. The information team works in the tents near each entrance, answering questions and handing out maps. You don't need any experience, but you do need to be patient, because people ask the same questions all day. The stewarding team manages crowds near the stages. This is the only team where we ask for previous experience, because safety is involved. The recycling team collects and sorts rubbish around the site. It's hard physical work, but people say it's the most fun. The artist team looks after the performers backstage, and that involves working late, often until two in the morning. And finally, the children's area team runs games and craft activities. Everyone in that team needs a police check, which we arrange for you, but it takes around four weeks, so please apply early. Applications close at the end of this month.

Lựa chọn ghép: A needs previous experience · B involves working late at night · C requires a police check · D needs a lot of patience · E involves hard physical work · F is the most popular team
• Volunteers must wear the festival T-shirt so that → visitors can recognise them. (✗ they can get free meals. · ✗ they can enter the campsite. · ✗ the organisers can count them.)
• What does the speaker say about the volunteers' campsite? → Volunteers must bring their own tent. (✗ It costs a small fee. · ✗ It is next to the main campsite. · ✗ It is completely quiet at night.)
• The online training must be completed → at least three days before the festival. (✗ on Saturday the twelfth of July. · ✗ after the first shift. · ✗ by the end of this month.)
• What is the minimum number of hours each volunteer works? → Twelve (✗ Six · ✗ Eighteen · ✗ Twenty)
• Applications to volunteer close → at the end of this month. (✗ on the twelfth of July. · ✗ four weeks before the festival. · ✗ when three hundred people have applied.)
• the information team → D needs a lot of patience
• the stewarding team → A needs previous experience
• the recycling team → E involves hard physical work
• the artist team → B involves working late at night
• the children's area team → C requires a police check
Part 3: Discussing a survey on students' sleep
Lời

A: Come in, Tom and Hana. You wanted to talk about your survey on students' sleep? B: Yes. We've collected the data, but we're not sure how to present it. A: How many responses did you get in the end? C: A hundred and twenty. We were hoping for two hundred, but it was exam season, so a lot of people didn't reply. A: That's still a reasonable sample. What surprised you most? B: I expected most students to sleep less than seven hours, and they did. What surprised me was how many said they slept badly even when they had time. Nearly half. C: For me, the surprise was caffeine. I assumed coffee would be the main problem, but energy drinks were far more common than we thought. A: Interesting. Did you ask about phones? C: We did. But almost everyone uses their phone in bed, so we can't compare people who do and people who don't. We can't really say anything about the effect. A: That's an honest conclusion, and it's better than claiming more than your data shows. Now, the presentation. What were you thinking? B: I wanted to put all the results in one big table. A: Tables are fine for the appendix, but in the talk you need something the audience can take in quickly. C: Maybe a bar chart for sleep hours, and a pie chart for the drinks? A: A bar chart, yes. But pie charts are hard to read when there are more than three or four categories. How many types of drink did you have? C: Seven. A: Then I'd use bars for that too. And include a few quotations from the open questions. They bring the numbers to life. B: We've got some great ones. One student said she drinks coffee to wake up and herbal tea to fall asleep. A: Perfect. Now, what will you each do before Friday? B: I'll redo the charts. C: And I'll write the section on limitations: the sample size and the timing. A: Who's choosing the quotations? B: We'll do that together, since we both read all the answers. A: And someone needs to check that nothing in the quotations could identify a student. C: I'll do that. I've already removed the names from the data. A: Good. And practise the talk at least twice. B: I'll book a room so we can practise together on Thursday.

Lựa chọn ghép: A Tom · B Hana · C both students
• Why did fewer students reply than expected? → It was during the exam period. (✗ The questionnaire was too long. · ✗ It was only available online. · ✗ Students were on holiday.)
• What surprised Tom most? → how many students slept badly despite having time (✗ how few students slept seven hours · ✗ how many students used phones in bed · ✗ how much coffee students drank)
• What surprised Hana? → Energy drinks were more common than expected. (✗ Few students drank coffee at night. · ✗ Most students avoided caffeine. · ✗ Coffee helped students sleep.)
• What do the students conclude about phone use? → Their data cannot show its effect. (✗ It is the main cause of poor sleep. · ✗ It has no effect on sleep. · ✗ Only a few students use phones in bed.)
• Why does the tutor advise against a pie chart for the drinks? → There are too many categories. (✗ Pie charts look unprofessional. · ✗ The audience prefers tables. · ✗ The numbers are too small.)
• redo the charts → A Tom
• write the section on limitations → B Hana
• choose the quotations → C both students
• check that the quotations are anonymous → B Hana
• book a room for practice → A Tom
Part 4: Lecture: kelp forests and sea otters
Lời

Good morning. Today I want to talk about one of the most productive habitats on the planet: kelp forests. Kelp is a type of large brown seaweed. It grows in cold, shallow water close to the coast, and in good conditions some species can grow up to half a metre in a single day. These underwater forests provide food and shelter for thousands of species, from tiny snails to fish and seals. Kelp forests also matter to humans. They protect coastlines by reducing the energy of waves during storms, and they support important fisheries. More recently, scientists have become interested in their role in storing carbon, although how much carbon kelp actually stores in the long term is still debated. To understand how these forests stay healthy, we need to look at a small animal: the sea urchin. Urchins feed on kelp, and when there are too many of them, they can destroy a forest, leaving what scientists call an urchin barren: bare rock with almost nothing growing on it. This is where sea otters come in. Otters eat urchins. An adult otter needs to eat about a quarter of its body weight every day, because it has no layer of fat to keep it warm. In the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, otters were hunted almost to extinction for their fur. As otter numbers fell, urchin numbers rose, and large areas of kelp disappeared. Where otters have returned, the kelp has often recovered. Ecologists call an animal like this a keystone species: one whose effect on its environment is far greater than its numbers would suggest. Today, kelp faces new threats, particularly warmer water. In northern California, for example, a marine heatwave, combined with the loss of a starfish that also eats urchins, led to the disappearance of most of the region's kelp. Restoration projects now include removing urchins by hand, with the help of volunteer divers.


• Kelp grows in cold, ______ water near the coast → shallow
• Some species can grow half a ______ in one day → metre / meter
• Kelp forests reduce the energy of ______ during storms → waves
• Scientists still debate how much ______ kelp stores → carbon
• Too many urchins leave bare ______ → rock
• Otters have no layer of ______ to keep them warm → fat
• Otters were once hunted for their ______ → fur
• An animal with a very large effect on its environment: a ______ species → keystone
• In northern California, the loss of a ______ that eats urchins added to the problem → starfish
• Urchins are now removed by hand with help from volunteer ______ → divers

🎓 Cambridge Nghe · nối người nói (v126) (9)

Kiểu B2 First Part 3 (1 nhiệm vụ) và C1 Advanced / C2 Proficiency Part 4 (2 nhiệm vụ). Mỗi bộ 5 người nói, 8 ý, 3 ý thừa. Kiểm: mỗi người khớp đúng một ý, ý thừa không khớp ai, bẫy từ ngữ hợp lý, độ khó hợp cấp.

Learning a musical instrument as an adult B2 · Năm người kể chuyện học nhạc cụ khi đã lớn
🗣 1. I'd always assumed that if I took one lesson a week, I'd be playing simple songs within a couple of months. My teacher was very polite about it, but after the third lesson she asked how much I'd practised, and the honest answer was about ten minutes. It turns out that twenty minutes a day, every day, is the minimum. Once I accepted that, things improved, but it took me a while to stop expecting results without the work.
  → D. I underestimated how much practice was needed.
🗣 2. My daughter started the violin at school, and I used to sit in the car during her lessons, scrolling through my phone. One day her teacher suggested I join in, just for fun. Now we practise together most evenings, and we've even played a duet at my mother's birthday. We were never particularly close when she was younger, and this has given us something that's just ours.
  → E. It brought me closer to a family member.
🗣 3. I work in a hospital emergency department, so my days are pretty intense. I took up the drums because a friend said hitting things would be good for me, and he was right. After a difficult shift, half an hour of drumming clears my head in a way nothing else does. I'm not especially talented, and I'll never play in a band, but that isn't the point. It's my way of letting go of the day.
  → A. It helped me deal with pressure at work.
🗣 4. I signed up for group guitar classes at the community centre, and when I walked in on the first evening, everyone else was about fifteen. I nearly turned round and went home. I felt so out of place, being the oldest by thirty years. But the teenagers were lovely. They'd show me chords and laugh at my jokes, even the bad ones. By the end of the course, I'd stopped noticing the age difference at all.
  → B. At first I felt awkward about being much older than the others.
🗣 5. I live in a very small flat, and I travel a lot for work, so a piano was never an option. I chose the ukulele because it's light enough to take anywhere. I've played it in hotel rooms all over the world, quietly, so I don't disturb anyone. Some of my friends think it's a toy rather than a real instrument, but I've learned to play some quite complicated pieces on it.
  → H. I chose my instrument because it was easy to carry.
What does each speaker say about learning the instrument?: A. It helped me deal with pressure at work. · B. At first I felt awkward about being much older than the others. · C. I had to change teachers to make progress. · D. I underestimated how much practice was needed. · E. It brought me closer to a family member. · F. I gave up for a while but later started again. · G. Playing in front of other people was the hardest part. · H. I chose my instrument because it was easy to carry.
Moving to a new city B2 · Năm người kể chuyện chuyển đến thành phố mới
🗣 1. When I got the job offer in the capital, the salary looked enormous compared with what I'd been earning. What nobody told me was that rent would take more than half of it. My first flat was a single room above a laundry, and by the end of each month I was living on noodles. It took me nearly two years to save enough to move somewhere decent.
  → B. The cost of living was higher than I'd expected.
🗣 2. I didn't know a soul when I arrived, and I mostly worked from home, so I could easily have gone weeks without talking to anyone. Then I saw a poster for a running club that met in the park on Sunday mornings. I'm a terrible runner, but the people were great, and most of my closest friends here are from that club.
  → C. A hobby helped me meet people.
🗣 3. My partner had been offered a research post at the university, and it was too good an opportunity for her to turn down. I'd lived in the same town all my life, so for me the move was a big step. I spent the first few months looking for work and feeling a bit lost, if I'm honest, but I've found a job I enjoy now.
  → F. I moved because of my partner's job.
🗣 4. People had warned me that big-city people are cold and unfriendly, so I expected to be ignored. In fact, on my first day, my neighbour knocked on the door with a plate of cakes, and the man at the corner shop learned my name within a week. I'm not saying everyone is like that, but it certainly wasn't what I'd been led to believe.
  → G. I was surprised by how friendly people were.
🗣 5. The first month was miserable. It rained constantly, I hated my job, and I had a ticket home booked for the end of the month. Then, two days before I was due to leave, my manager moved me to a different team, and suddenly everything changed. I cancelled the ticket, and that was six years ago.
  → H. I nearly went back home in the first month.
What does each speaker say about moving to the city?: A. I found it hard to make friends at work. · B. The cost of living was higher than I'd expected. · C. A hobby helped me meet people. · D. I missed the food from home. · E. I often got lost at first. · F. I moved because of my partner's job. · G. I was surprised by how friendly people were. · H. I nearly went back home in the first month.
A job they did as students B2 · Năm người kể về việc làm thêm thời sinh viên
🗣 1. I worked at the complaints desk of a department store during the holidays. Some days people would shout at me about things that had nothing to do with me, like the weather or the parking. I learned not to take it personally, to listen first and then offer something practical. Those skills have been surprisingly useful in every job I've had since.
  → E. I learned how to deal with difficult customers.
🗣 2. I spent a summer picking fruit on a farm. I thought it would be a relaxing way to earn some money outdoors, but by the end of the first day my back was aching and my hands were covered in scratches. You're bending over for eight hours in the sun. I was fitter than I'd ever been by the end, though.
  → G. It was harder physically than I'd imagined.
🗣 3. I was studying law, and I took a part-time job as a teaching assistant at a primary school, mainly for the money. I didn't expect to enjoy it so much. Seeing a child suddenly understand something was more satisfying than anything in my law books. I finished my degree, but then I trained as a teacher, and I've never regretted it.
  → C. It led me to choose a different career.
🗣 4. I had two jobs while I was at university, one in a café and one doing deliveries, as well as my studies. At first it was chaos. I missed deadlines and once fell asleep in a lecture. Eventually I started planning every week on a big calendar on my wall, and that habit has stayed with me. I still can't work without a plan.
  → A. It taught me to organise my time.
🗣 5. I worked nights at a hotel reception desk. It was quiet most of the time, and the other receptionist, a woman from Brazil, and I used to talk for hours about everything. That was twenty years ago. She came to my wedding, I went to hers, and we still call each other every month.
  → F. I made a friend for life.
What does each speaker say about the job?: A. It taught me to organise my time. · B. I was treated unfairly by my employer. · C. It led me to choose a different career. · D. I earned more than I had expected. · E. I learned how to deal with difficult customers. · F. I made a friend for life. · G. It was harder physically than I'd imagined. · H. I only did it because my parents insisted.
Why they started volunteering C1 · Năm người kể vì sao đi làm tình nguyện
🗣 1. When my father lost his job, it was a food bank that kept us going for months, and I never forgot the volunteers there, how they treated us with dignity. So when I was earning again, it felt only right to give something back. What surprised me was how complicated the issue of food poverty is. I'd assumed it was mostly about people being unemployed, but many of the people we help have jobs; they just don't earn enough. That's changed how I talk about it.
  → NV1: C. to repay help they had once received · NV2: E. a more realistic view of the problem
🗣 2. I'd just retired, and frankly, I was climbing the walls. After forty years of busy working days, I had nothing to fill the hours. A neighbour mentioned that the hospital needed drivers to take patients to appointments, so I signed up. I've always been a rather impatient person, as my children would tell you, but sitting in waiting rooms for hours has taught me to slow down. I hardly recognise myself.
  → NV1: D. because they had too much free time · NV2: C. more patience
🗣 3. I needed a placement for my social work degree, it was compulsory, and I ended up at a homeless shelter. I went in feeling rather pleased with myself for helping. What struck me, though, was how much luck had been on my side all along: a family to fall back on, a safety net I'd never even noticed. I'm still volunteering there, two years after the placement ended.
  → NV1: G. because their course required it · NV2: H. an awareness of their own advantages
🗣 4. I'd moved to the city for work and didn't know anyone. A colleague suggested the conservation group that spends weekends clearing rubbish from the river, and I went along mostly hoping to make some friends. What I hadn't expected was how much I'd learn: how to use the tools properly, how to identify plants, even how to repair a small boat. Those are things I'd never have picked up in an office.
  → NV1: E. to meet people after moving · NV2: A. practical skills
🗣 5. I saw a documentary about adult literacy and was shocked by how many people struggle to read a simple form. I volunteered as a reading tutor the following week. At first I was terrified of the group sessions, because I'd always hated speaking in front of people, but having to explain things clearly to a room of adults has done more for my nerves than any course could. I've even given a talk at the library.
  → NV1: H. because of something they saw on television · NV2: G. confidence in speaking to groups

Starting their own business C1 · Năm người kể chuyện tự mở doanh nghiệp
🗣 1. It started with a few candles I made for friends at Christmas. They kept asking for more, and then friends of friends did, until my kitchen was full of wax. When I finally turned it into a business, the hardest thing by far was deciding what to charge. Charge too little and you're working for nothing; charge too much and people walk away. I changed my prices four times in the first year.
  → NV1: C. a hobby that grew · NV2: F. setting the right price
🗣 2. When the factory closed, two hundred of us lost our jobs on the same day. I'd been a mechanic there for fifteen years, so I opened a small repair workshop with my redundancy money. The work itself has been fine. What I wasn't prepared for was being on my own all day. At the factory there was always someone to have a laugh with. Now it's just me and the radio.
  → NV1: A. losing their job · NV2: D. working alone
🗣 3. I live in a town full of elderly people, and I noticed there was nobody offering to help them with technology: setting up phones, video-calling grandchildren, that kind of thing. So I started doing it. Demand has never been the problem. The difficulty is that the big electronics chains have noticed the same thing and started offering similar help in their shops. A small business like mine simply can't match their size.
  → NV1: B. noticing a need nobody was meeting · NV2: C. competing with large companies
🗣 4. I'd worked for the same manager for six years, and he never listened to a single idea I had. In the end I thought: if I'm going to work this hard, it might as well be for myself. So I started a small catering company. The clients pay well, but often sixty or ninety days after the event, while I have to pay for food and staff immediately. There were months when I genuinely didn't know if I could pay the rent.
  → NV1: F. frustration with a former boss · NV2: B. waiting to be paid
🗣 5. I've got three young children, and an office job with fixed hours just wasn't working any more. So I set up as a freelance translator. I can choose when I work, which is wonderful. The downside is that the office is the kitchen table, and the children don't understand that when I'm at the laptop, I'm not available. Clients have heard a toddler screaming during video calls more times than I'd like to admit.
  → NV1: D. wanting more flexible hours · NV2: H. keeping work and family life apart

Nhiệm vụ 2 · Task 2: What has been each speaker's biggest difficulty?: A. finding reliable staff · B. waiting to be paid · C. competing with large companies · D. working alone · E. dealing with regulations · F. setting the right price · G. advertising the business · H. keeping work and family life apart
Learning a foreign language as an adult C1 · Năm người kể chuyện học ngoại ngữ khi đã lớn
🗣 1. My wife's family is from Seville, and for the first years of our marriage I'd sit at family dinners smiling and understanding nothing. I wanted to be able to joke with her father, not just nod at him. Apps didn't do much for me, to be honest. What changed everything was a weekly conversation evening at a bar near my office. Two hours of talking, making mistakes, and nobody minding.
  → NV1: A. to communicate with their partner's family · NV2: D. a regular conversation group
🗣 2. After I retired, my doctor said something about keeping the brain busy, and learning a language seemed more interesting than crosswords. I chose Italian. The grammar books were hopeless for me; I'd forget everything by the next day. Then my granddaughter lent me some of her picture books. Simple sentences, lots of repetition, and pictures to help. I'm now on to proper novels, slowly.
  → NV1: E. to keep their mind active · NV2: F. books written for children
🗣 3. At school my French teacher told me I had no ear for languages, and I believed her for twenty years. Then one day I thought, why should she have the last word? I started learning Japanese, of all things. I write a few lines every evening about my day, in Japanese, and once a week a language partner online corrects them. Seeing my mistakes in writing helps them stick.
  → NV1: H. to prove someone wrong · NV2: G. writing a daily diary
🗣 4. I got hooked on a Korean drama during lockdown, and after months of reading subtitles I wanted to understand it properly. I started picking up phrases almost by accident. Then I switched the subtitles to Korean as well as the sound, so I could see and hear the words at the same time. That's been far more useful than any textbook.
  → NV1: F. because of a television series · NV2: E. watching with subtitles
🗣 5. My company was opening an office in Brazil, and the manager's job there required Portuguese. I had six months. I'm too shy for conversation groups, so I did something that probably looks a bit mad: I described everything I was doing out loud, in Portuguese, while I cooked, cleaned and drove to work. It forced me to find the words I was missing. I got the job.
  → NV1: B. to get a better job · NV2: H. talking aloud to themselves

Changing career in mid-life C2 · Năm người kể chuyện đổi nghề giữa đời
🗣 1. I spent fifteen years in advertising, much of it selling things to children that their parents couldn't really afford. For a long time I told myself that everybody does it, but the justification wore thinner every year, until one campaign for a sugary cereal felt like the last straw. I retrained as a primary teacher. The work is infinitely more rewarding, but I won't pretend the salary isn't a shock. We've sold the second car, and holidays are camping trips now.
  → NV1: H. growing doubts about the ethics of their work · NV2: C. the drop in income
🗣 2. A minor heart problem at forty-four, nothing dramatic in the end, but it made me take stock. I'd always loved woodwork, so I enrolled on a furniture-making course. What I hadn't bargained for was how hard it is to be bad at something. I'd forgotten what it feels like not to know how to do anything. Cutting a straight line took me weeks, and I went home most evenings feeling about twelve years old.
  → NV1: A. a health scare · NV2: G. learning to be a beginner again
🗣 3. I was a corporate lawyer, and I'd never considered anything else until I got chatting to a stranger on a delayed train who turned out to run a vineyard. By the time we reached London I'd more or less decided. I now work for a winemaker in Portugal, and the hardest adjustment has been the hierarchy. My supervisor is twenty-six, and she's perfectly entitled to tell me how to do my job, which she does without hesitation.
  → NV1: C. a chance conversation · NV2: B. taking instructions from someone younger
🗣 4. Once our youngest left for university, the house was suddenly very quiet, and I realised I'd organised my whole life around school runs and timetables. I left my job at the bank to try freelance illustration. Nobody warned me how disorientating it is to have no one telling you when to start or stop. Some days I achieve a great deal; others simply dissolve into coffee and good intentions.
  → NV1: F. their children leaving home · NV2: H. the lack of structure in the day
🗣 5. I'd wanted to be a nurse since I was a child, but my family needed me to earn money, so I went into insurance and stayed for twenty years. When the mortgage was finally paid off, there was nothing stopping me any more. What genuinely surprised me was how some old friends reacted, as though I'd announced I was joining a cult. A few have drifted away. The ones who stayed have been wonderful.
  → NV1: G. a long-held ambition finally becoming possible · NV2: E. the reaction of friends

Living abroad C2 · Năm người kể chuyện sống ở nước ngoài
🗣 1. In my own language I'm the person who makes the jokes at dinner, but in German I was reduced to someone who could only discuss the weather and train times. It was oddly painful, feeling that people were meeting a duller version of me. Eight years on, I'm fluent, and the strange thing is that when I go back to Lisbon now, I feel like a visitor there too. I'm no longer certain which place I'd call home.
  → NV1: E. being unable to express their personality · NV2: B. they are less sure where home is
🗣 2. Nothing prepared me for the paperwork. To open a bank account I needed an address; to rent a flat I needed a bank account. It took four months, three visits to the same office and a great deal of patience to break the circle. Ironically, it's made me far more appreciative of the system back home, which I used to complain about constantly. At least there, the forms are online and somebody answers the phone.
  → NV1: A. the bureaucracy · NV2: D. they appreciate its public services more
🗣 3. In Brazil, a dinner invitation for eight o'clock means people start arriving around half past nine, and I spent my first months sitting alone in tidy flats wondering whether I'd got the day wrong. I've relaxed about that now. What I hadn't expected was to see my own country differently. Back in the Netherlands, we pride ourselves on being honest, but I now realise how blunt we can sound. My Brazilian friends find us quite alarming.
  → NV1: G. different attitudes to time · NV2: E. they realise how direct its people are
🗣 4. During the week, work kept me busy, but Sundays in Seoul were long and silent. Everybody seemed to be with family, and I'd wander round shopping centres just to be among people. Living there made me realise how much I'd taken my village for granted: the way neighbours drop in uninvited, the endless gossip at the shop. I used to find it suffocating; now I miss it.
  → NV1: F. loneliness at weekends · NV2: G. they value its sense of community
🗣 5. Coming from Kerala to Norway, I'd prepared myself for the cold, but not for the dark. In December the sun barely rises. I took vitamin pills, bought a special lamp and gradually adjusted. Norwegians repair things, share things and recycle with an almost religious seriousness, and since living here I can't visit my family without noticing how much we throw away. I bite my tongue, mostly.
  → NV1: B. the climate · NV2: F. they notice how wasteful it is

Failure at work C2 · Năm người kể về một lần thất bại trong nghề
🗣 1. As a young scientist I published a result I was very proud of, and then two other labs tried to reproduce it and couldn't. It turned out the effect came from a contaminated batch of chemicals. I felt humiliated at the time, but it made me a much better researcher. Now, whenever a result looks too neat, my first question is what I might be taking for granted.
  → NV1: D. a research result that could not be repeated · NV2: B. to question their assumptions
🗣 2. My first novel was turned down by twenty-three publishers. For a while each rejection felt like a verdict on me as a person, which is absurd when you think about it. What eventually changed was that I began to see the manuscript as a thing I had made, rather than a piece of myself. The book that finally got published was my third, and the rejections that came before it stung far less.
  → NV1: F. a book that was rejected many times · NV2: C. to separate who they are from what they make
🗣 3. Twenty minutes into the most important concert of my career, the power failed and the stage went dark. I stood there frozen, with eight hundred people waiting. Eventually the orchestra carried on from memory, but I've never forgotten the panic. These days I rehearse for disasters: what we'll do if a string breaks, if a soloist falls ill, if the lights go. It sounds pessimistic, but it's actually what lets me relax on stage.
  → NV1: B. a performance that went badly wrong · NV2: G. to plan for things going wrong
🗣 4. My business partner and I had been friends since university, and we ran the company together for nine years before it fell apart over money. Looking back, the problems were obvious for at least two years, but I was too proud to admit I couldn't solve them alone. If I'd brought in an adviser, or even just talked to someone honestly, I suspect we'd still be in business, and still be friends.
  → NV1: E. a business partnership that collapsed · NV2: A. to ask for help earlier
🗣 5. We spent eighteen months developing a smart water bottle that reminded you to drink. Every investor loved it; every customer ignored it. The few honest reviewers who said it solved a problem nobody had were dismissed by our team as people who didn't get it. They got it perfectly well. Now, when someone tells me an idea won't work, I make myself take the objection seriously before I defend the idea.
  → NV1: A. a product that nobody bought · NV2: E. to listen to critics

Nhiệm vụ 2 · Task 2: What did each speaker learn from the failure?: A. to ask for help earlier · B. to question their assumptions · C. to separate who they are from what they make · D. to trust their instincts · E. to listen to critics · F. that luck plays a large part · G. to plan for things going wrong · H. to celebrate small successes

💼 TOEIC Part 1 · tả tranh (v127) (12)

Đề thật dùng ảnh chụp; app dùng tranh vẽ đơn giản (emoji trên iPhone trông khác một chút). Kiểm: tranh có cho thấy rõ câu đúng không, 3 câu sai có sai rõ với tranh không (nhất là bẫy is being / has been), tiếng Anh tự nhiên.

t1-01

A. A plant is being watered.
B. A person is working on a laptop. ✅
C. A person is hanging a picture on the wall.
D. Some chairs are stacked by the window.
Người đang ngồi làm việc với máy tính xách tay. Cây có trong tranh nhưng không ai tưới (is being watered); tranh đã treo sẵn, không ai đang treo.
t1-02

A. Some bicycles have been parked in a row. ✅
B. A man is riding a bicycle.
C. A tree is being cut down.
D. The bicycles are being repaired.
Xe đã được dựng thành hàng (have been parked). Không có người trong tranh nên câu "is riding", "are being repaired" (đang có người sửa) đều sai.
t1-03

A. A waiter is serving a customer.
B. Some dishes are being washed.
C. A cook is preparing food. ✅
D. The kitchen is empty.
Đầu bếp đang nấu ăn. Không có khách hay người phục vụ; không ai rửa bát; bếp có người nên không "empty".
t1-04

A. Two people are sitting at a table.
B. A man is making a phone call.
C. They are exchanging business cards.
D. Two people are shaking hands. ✅
Hai người đang bắt tay. Họ đứng, không ngồi; không gọi điện; "exchanging business cards" là bẫy hay gặp khi tranh có hai người gặp nhau.
t1-05

A. Boxes have been stacked on the shelves. ✅
B. A worker is opening a box.
C. The shelves are empty.
D. Boxes are being loaded onto a truck.
Thùng đã được xếp lên kệ (have been stacked). Không có người nên "is opening", "are being loaded" sai; kệ có thùng nên không "empty".
t1-06

A. People are swimming in the water.
B. Some boats are floating near a pier. ✅
C. A boat is being painted.
D. A bridge crosses the river.
Thuyền nổi trên mặt nước gần cầu tàu (pier). Không có người bơi hay sơn thuyền; cầu tàu không phải cây cầu bắc qua sông (bridge).
t1-07

A. A woman is writing a letter.
B. Students are leaving the room.
C. A woman is standing in front of a board. ✅
D. Chairs are being stacked.
Người phụ nữ đứng trước bảng. Không có học sinh; ghế đặt trên sàn, không ai đang chồng ghế (are being stacked).
t1-08

A. People are waiting at a bus stop. ✅
B. A bus is being repaired.
C. Passengers are getting off a train.
D. The road is closed to traffic.
Hai người đứng chờ ở trạm xe buýt. Không ai sửa xe; tranh không có tàu hoả (train); đường có xe chạy nên không "closed".
t1-09

A. Some people are eating dinner.
B. A waiter is pouring drinks.
C. The plates are being washed.
D. A table has been set for a meal. ✅
Bàn đã được bày sẵn đĩa, ly (has been set). Không có người nên không ai đang ăn, rót đồ uống hay rửa đĩa.
t1-10

A. A worker is carrying a ladder.
B. A worker is standing on a ladder. ✅
C. A wall is being knocked down.
D. Some tools have been left on a chair.
Người thợ đứng trên thang. "Carrying a ladder" là bẫy (có thang nhưng không mang vác); hộp dụng cụ ở dưới sàn, không trên ghế.
t1-11

A. A person is sitting on a bench.
B. A fountain is being installed.
C. A person is running along a path. ✅
D. Some leaves are being swept.
Một người đang chạy trên lối đi. Không có ghế đá; đài phun nước đã có sẵn, không ai đang lắp (is being installed); không ai quét lá.
t1-12

A. A shopper is paying at the register.
B. The shelves are being stocked.
C. A cart is being pushed out of a store.
D. Some items have been placed in a cart. ✅
Trong xe đẩy đã có hàng (have been placed). Không có người nên không ai trả tiền, xếp hàng lên kệ (are being stocked) hay đẩy xe.

🖼 Starters (Pre-A1) · từ có tranh (v128) (79)

Mỗi dòng: tranh (emoji, trên iPhone là emoji Apple), từ, cụm dùng trong câu "This is …" (trống = không đếm được), câu nghe. Kiểm: tranh đúng là vật đó, không lẫn với từ khác trong nhóm, câu nghe tự nhiên với trẻ.
